#7023. Senior Manager, Business Operations at Clutch

Clutch is at a pivotal moment. We're growing significantly year over year, and we need more connective tissue to keep pace. We're hiring a Senior Manager, Business Operations to connect the commercial side of the house, so the teams that win, onboard, and retain our customers work from the same facts and toward the same goals. This role sits in Finance.

This is a people manager role. You'll lead our Revenue Operations (RevOps) team and act as an operating partner to senior leaders across Sales, Marketing, Customer Success, Implementation, People, and Finance. Together with the RevOps team, you own the shared language of the business (definitions, data, and processes) so commercial teams make decisions from one view of it.

When something important doesn't have an owner yet, it lands with you: a partnership strategy, a new commercial model, or pricing and margin guidance for leadership. This is a fast-moving startup. We're building toward a much larger company but we aren't one yet, and most of this role has no playbook. You'll define the process, build the tool, and hand it to the team. If you want established structure, this likely isn't the role.

You'll report to the Director of Business Operations and work directly with senior leaders across the company as a neutral partner to every function rather than an advocate for one. Your work will be visible, and it will significantly influence how we make commercial decisions.

You'll lead a small, high-ownership RevOps team that runs the systems and data our commercial teams depend on, and creates leverage for them to do their jobs even better. The team doesn't just own systems. They build automation, tooling, and processes, and they're close partners to Sales and Marketing. You'll coach them, set priorities, and open up more strategic scope, while giving them real autonomy.

Learn the business end to end: the revenue funnel, the customer lifecycle, how each commercial team works day to day, and where decisions get stuck

Build trust with your RevOps team: learn the work they own, and set priorities and team rhythms together

Deliver the first version of client-level margin and cost-to-serve, the analysis that grounds our pricing strategy

Develop a first point of view on pricing and packaging for revenue leadership, built on that analysis

Put AI to work in your own workflow from week one, and identify the first set of manual, repetitive processes across the commercial teams worth automating

Stand up a first version of a deal desk, including an approval workflow, pricing and discount guardrails, and standard commercial terms

Partner with Sales and Marketing leaders as they prepare for the monthly business review: pressure-test their plans and point out risks, then solve them with the RevOps team by creating new processes, tooling, or automation

Build on the strength of the RevOps team: prioritize its roadmap against business outcomes and take on more strategic work together

Launch your first AI-enabled workflows or automations that remove manual reporting or reconciliation for the commercial teams, with time saved tracked

Have a team whose output and standards hold, with the processes, tooling, and automation you've built running without you in the room

Have AI-enabled, self-serve tooling that commercial teams use day to day, with measurable time savings

Be the person leadership trusts to pick up the next important thing that doesn't have an owner yet

5-8 years of total experience that combines consulting or banking with time as an operator inside a company

Experience managing people: you've coached ICs, given direct feedback, made tough talent calls, and built team rhythms

Startup experience. You've worked in a fast-moving, ever-changing environment where structure wasn't already there, and you built it. This is not a role for someone whose experience is only at large, established enterprises.

Strong analytical and financial modeling skills, including forecasting. You're very comfortable building models in spreadsheets from scratch, and they hold up to scrutiny. You use AI to move faster and sharpen your work, and you have the foundational modeling knowledge to know when its output is wrong.

Data-first and self-sufficient. You open the dataset before you build the slide, you're always looking for more data to test an idea, and you're comfortable getting into raw data yourself. You use AI to analyze it faster and know enough to check its work.

Commercial acumen: you can read commercial terms, spot the problematic ones, and reason about pricing, discounting, margin, and deal structure

A clear communicator who can walk an executive through a messy problem and leave them with a decision, and who is just as effective working alongside an AE, a CSM, or an engineer

Comfort with ambiguity. When something isn't set up yet, your instinct is to build it, not to wait for it. You create structure, manage competing priorities, and pivot when things change.

Relentless follow-through: you close loops, and people stop checking on what you've said you'll do

A partner's temperament: you challenge constructively without taking sides, you surface problems early, and you resolve cross-team conflict without escalating it

Industry experience in fintech, financial services, lending, banking, or credit unions

Please note that this role may evolve as our business needs change, so we appreciate your flexibility and adaptability.

As a champion of financial well-being, we address the urgent need for affordable lending solutions in an era where the average American grapples with over $155,000 in household debt.  Unlike traditional financial institutions, Clutch develops software to turn Credit Unions into FinTech lenders and leverage their balance sheets to responsibly lend to over 130M Americans. Our mission extends beyond mere financial transactions; we strive to fundamentally enhance the way credit unions interact with their members. By integrating cutting-edge technologies and user-centric designs, we help credit unions provide seamless digital experiences that are on par with leading tech companies. This approach not only preserves but revitalizes the longstanding tradition of community and member-focused service inherent to credit unions.

#7018. Gartner: Worldwide AI Spending to Reach $2.67 Trillion in 2026

The artificial intelligence industry is expected to generate $2.67 trillion in worldwide spending this year, according to a new forecast from Gartner. But relatively little of that money will be spent on the generative AI models dominating the industry's headlines.

The much bigger business is everything required to make them run.

Worldwide spending on AI infrastructure will reach nearly $1.5 trillion in 2026, accounting for about 56% of all AI spending. Gartner expects spending on generative AI models themselves to total just $28.3 billion. Put another way, for every dollar Gartner expects to be spent on generative AI models this year, more than $52 will be spent on AI infrastructure.

It is a striking measure of how the economics of the AI boom are changing. ChatGPT, Claude, Gemini, and other models may be the most visible products of the generative AI era, but much of the money is going to the enormous buildout of servers, semiconductors, networks, cloud capacity, and other infrastructure required to support them.

"The buildout of AI data center capacity is the largest infrastructure project humanity has ever undertaken," John-David Lovelock, distinguished vice president analyst at Gartner, said in announcing the forecast.

Gartner now expects worldwide AI spending to increase 49.5% this year, from about $1.79 trillion in 2025 to $2.67 trillion in 2026. Infrastructure represents $1.484 trillion of that total. The next-largest categories are AI services, at $576.5 billion, and AI software, at $461.6 billion. Spending on AI agents and assistants is expected to reach $29.2 billion, only slightly more than the $28.3 billion Gartner forecasts for generative AI models.

The numbers also show just how quickly expectations are changing. In January, Gartner forecast $2.53 trillion in total AI spending for 2026, including $1.37 trillion for infrastructure. By May, those forecasts had risen to $2.60 trillion and $1.43 trillion, respectively. The latest forecast raises them again, to $2.67 trillion overall and $1.48 trillion for infrastructure.

Since January, Gartner has added roughly $143 billion to its 2026 AI spending estimate. About $118 billion of that increase, or roughly 83%, comes from a higher infrastructure forecast.

That is significant because infrastructure was already, by far, the largest component of Gartner's AI spending estimate. As the firm's expectations for the AI market have risen during 2026, most of the additional money has accumulated there.

The infrastructure category is broad. Gartner includes AI-optimized cloud infrastructure, servers, networking, AI processors, and devices. Demand for those systems remains strong despite rising memory prices, Lovelock said.

"The capacity growth from hyperscalers and service providers purchasing AI-optimized servers will continue to be the largest single area of spending," he said.

The federal Education Freedom Tax Credit (EFTC) is a new source of funding for education that could benefit 90% of students in states that have opted in to the program. However, some confusion and misconceptions exist surrounding details of the program, including which programs may qualify, how parents can apply for scholarships, and how public school students can benefit.

Class CrunchLabs, the suite of free curriculum resources from NASA engineer turned YouTube science communicator Mark Rober, has released its first two courses on YouTube.

AI agents have moved into mainstream enterprise use, but the content infrastructure needed to support them has struggled to keep up, according to a new report from cloud content management company Box.

Enterprise AI projects are running into infrastructure questions as organizations move from planning toward deployment, according to recent research from Digital Realty.

#7016. Employers want AI skills. Here’s how colleges are adding them without new hires.

Ask a college president what students need to graduate ready for work, and AI is now near the top of the list. More than a third of entry-level jobs now require AI skills, nearly triple the share six months earlier. Yet 63% of faculty say recent graduates aren't prepared to use AI in the workplace.

Most academic leaders agree AI belongs in the curriculum. The harder question is who will teach it, especially at small, tuition-dependent institutions.

AI adds hurdles most new programs don't face:

So many institutions stall, and students graduate without the skills employers are hiring for.

Rize Education helps colleges lower costs and grow enrollment through a shared program model, letting them launch high-demand, career-focused programs faster and for far less than building them alone. Its two AI programs have been adopted 94 times across the network of institutions.

With more than 1,800 students enrolled through these courses, Rize Education can finally answer the question provosts ask most: do students actually value an online AI course?

Rize surveys students at the middle and end of every course. Here’s what they said:

Students rate these courses as highly as others. 89% of Applied AI responses and 83% of AI Literacy responses rate the course as good as or better than other online courses students have taken. Against on-campus courses, the figures are 76% and 73%.

Limited AI experience didn’t hold students back. AI Literacy students rated their prior familiarity with AI just 3.9 out of 7, yet rated their confidence in finishing with a good grade 6.2 out of 7.

A well-run course shapes the experience. Students gave their highest marks to clear assignment instructions, reasonable grading and helpful instructors. As one AI Literacy student put it: "This course is challenging but all of the assignments work together to give a clear, functional foundational knowledge of AI that can immediately be put to use in professional and private settings."

For more than 1,300 students, an AI course was their first Rize-powered course, a sign these programs draw students in rather than just checking a box.

Employers are asking for AI skills, and students are rating these courses as highly as anything else they take. The full results break down what 1,800 students said about both programs.

Read the full results from Rize's AI programs.

#7012. Researchers and faculty sue NSF over axing of its social science unit

Let Higher Ed Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

The federal agency said it would close the unit in fiscal 2027, in what the lawsuit described as a unilateral decision that violated Administrative Procedure Act.

Since April, NSF has “stopped funding opportunities for basic SBE research,” reassigned almost all of SBE's staff and cut ties with scientists who were working with the directorate, according to Monday’s lawsuit.

President Donald Trump also fired every member of SBE’s governing board, who plaintiffs said had been kept in the dark about the agency’s future since he retook office almost two years ago.

Their lawsuit argued that NSF breached the APA by failing to consider the outcome of doing away with SBE — namely, that the agency would be unable to fulfill its mandate to “initiate and support basic scientific research and programs” in the social sciences.

Each year, SBE distributes some $247 million in grants for research into the social, behavioral and economic sciences — nearly two-thirds of all federal grants in these fields, according to NSF.

“Without that funding, hundreds of promising research projects directed to the pressing problems of our time — from the use and effects of artificial intelligence to child development in the age of social media — will never proceed,” the lawsuit said. Thousands of people also will lose their jobs, it said.

The plaintiffs also said NSF has given no official rationale for why it decided to extinguish the directorate.

In response to question from The Atlantic, a White House spokesperson said in a May statement that the Trump administration “is committed to cementing America’s dominance in cutting-edge technologies of the future — innovation that is being driven by advancements in hard sciences, not in ideologically-driven ‘social sciences.’”

But the administration never said why it is labeling entire fields such as psychology and economics as “ideologically driven,” the plaintiffs said in their lawsuit Monday. And even if it were to explain, they argued, the rationale would directly conflict with the statutes that created NSF, “which repeatedly direct the Foundation to support the social sciences.”

All of this has occurred with a significant power vacuum at the top levels of NSF. The agency has been without a permanent leader for more than a year and a half, after its last director, Sethuraman Panchanathan, resigned in April 2025. It is also without a deputy director.

Both positions require a presidential appointment and Senate confirmation. Trump’s pick for NSF director, former U.S. Department of Health and Human Services Deputy Secretary Jim O'Neill, has not been given a confirmation hearing.

NSF Chief of Staff Brian Stone, a defendant in Monday’s lawsuit, is currently performing the duties of the agency’s director. The vast majority of NSF's significant cuts and the corresponding tumult occurred during Stone’s tenure. He is set to retire at the end of the year.

“NSF cannot shut down the government’s largest source of funding for an entire scientific sector without a Director and without consulting the body Congress created to make such Decisions,” Monday’s lawsuit said. “And it certainly cannot do so with no explanation.”

Alongside two named college instructors, the organizations suing NSF are the Federation of Associations in Behavioral and Brain Sciences, the Society of Experimental Social Psychology, and the Social & Affective Neuroscience Society. The plaintiffs are represented by the advocacy nonprofit Democracy Forward.

#7009. Avantis Education Adds New VR Math and Language Learning Experiences

Avantis Education, the creator of ClassVR, has introduced two new immersive learning libraries for its Eduverse+ platform: EscapeRooms, a math suite for K-8 students, and LingoLab, a Spanish and English language learning suite for students in grades 6-12.

EscapeRooms features five themed VR experiences in which students solve puzzles and answer rapid-fire questions to race against the clock and break free of a themed escape room. Designed for use with ClassVR headsets, the suite offers two-, four-, and eight-minute challenges — with themes such as "Shark Lab," "The Floor Is Lava," and "cursed Egyptian Tomb" — that help students build core skills and master complex mathematical concepts. The challenges are available in eight languages and can be adapted for different age ranges. Each Escape Room also includes a teacher's guide with starter activities and professional development materials.

LingoLab provides more than 60 learning experiences in immersive formats such as 360-degree videos and 3D models, designed to support Spanish and English language learning from beginner to upper-intermediate levels. Lessons are aligned with ACTFL proficiency guidelines and WIDA English Language Development standards, as well as CEFR and the Oxford 3000.

"With Eduverse+, our focus is on giving teachers immersive content that has a clear role within everyday teaching and learning. Listening to educators is a vital part of that process, and both EscapeRooms and LingoLab were shaped by feedback from schools and real classroom needs," said Manjinder Kaur, vice president of product for Avantis Education, in a statement. "While they are very different experiences, both are designed to give students meaningful opportunities to develop skills, receive feedback, and build confidence. By continuing to expand Eduverse+, we're giving schools more ways to use virtual reality technology across the curriculum and making immersive learning relevant to an even wider range of classroom needs."

EscapeRooms and LingoLab join the existing Eduverse+ library of content suites, including EduverseAI, WildWorld, STEAM3D, and CareerHub. The new libraries are available to EduVerse+ customers at no additional cost.

For more information, visit the ClassVR site here.

#7006. Boston University misses revenue projections by $42M

Let Higher Ed Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

The private institution will have to change “on a much broader scale” rather than relying on one-time cuts, its CFO said Monday.

BU has already gone through painful cuts as it has looked to shrink expenses. Last year, officials announced plans to lay off 120 staff members while eliminating another 120 vacancies. The cuts were an effort to shave 5% from the university’s budget during the previous fiscal year.

“Our prior cuts were effective in giving us time for thoughtful transformation,” Tirella said Monday.

But officials say those sorts of measures aren’t enough to fix today’s issues. Instead, BU is looking at making “better use” of its facilities, standardizing some operations and reducing duplicating functions, said Chris Sedore, BU’s chief transformation officer.

Manuel Cuevas-Trisán, BU’s senior vice president who oversees HR, suggested the university could upskill and promote employees as it tries to make the most of its resources. “Each vacancy can become an opportunity to think about how the work is done,” he said.

BU officials are also mulling the institution’s offerings. Sedore said some recently launched master’s programs are performing well while others face low enrollment.

The revenue miss for fiscal 2027 follows challenges from the previous year. Last October, BU President Melissa Gilliam and other senior leaders announced a $30 million budget shortfall amid missed revenue and lower-than-expected graduate student enrollment. Leaders at the time sought to address the gap with one-time savings, which Tirella said are no longer enough to address the university’s fiscal challenges.

Those efforts helped shore up the budget overall. With both tuition and grant revenue sliding, BU’s overall fiscal 2026 revenue fell 1.4% year over year to $2.7 billion, but it preserved an operating surplus, according to its fiscal latest financials. However, that net surplus plummeted by 91.6% year over year to $4 million.

Despite fiscal challenges, BU has planned salary increases for employees this fiscal year, as well as spending on deferred maintenance and technology, officials said.

Tirella said BU’s endowment and reserves are providing the university a stable financial foundation. But they cannot fully address operating budget challenges, given restrictions on most endowment funds and fixed spending limits to maintain the endowment’s health over the long run.

“Our balance sheet, including cash reserves and the endowment, cannot sustainably offset ongoing declines in operating margins,” she said.

#7003. Study: AI Is Reshaping Work While Skills Erosion Remains Top Concern

AI is changing which human capabilities matter most at work, yet employees are reporting that some of those same capabilities are being eroded, according to a new study from IBM. Critical thinking, judgment and the ability to evaluate AI output sit at the center of that tension.

The IBM Institute for Business Value study, "Designing the Thinking Organization," is based on two surveys conducted with Oxford Economics from April through June 2026. IBM surveyed 1,500 CHROs and senior executives responsible for workforce strategy across 21 geographies and 23 industries, along with 8,800 full-time employees across 28 countries.

The chart below, created by ChatGPT from IBM's data, summarizes the executive-employee differences around critical thinking and AI oversight along with several of the report's other findings. The graphic also includes IBM's findings on skill erosion, CHRO participation in AI strategy, workflow definitions, AI adoption inside HR, and reinvestment of AI productivity gains.

The findings extend beyond HR. More than half of employees surveyed — 52% — said their assigned tasks changed during the past year as AI reshaped day-to-day responsibilities. Yet only 26% of organizations clearly define work across human-led, AI-assisted, and AI-executed activities. IBM characterizes the situation as work changing faster than organizations are redesigning it, with day-to-day adaptation often taking the place of deliberate decisions about how people and AI should work together.

IBM found broad agreement between executives and employees on the importance of critical thinking and problem framing. Fifty-seven percent of executives and 49% of employees placed those capabilities near the top of the skills needed for AI-enabled work. A much larger divide emerged around evaluating AI itself: Seventy-one percent of executives prioritize the ability to supervise, validate, or override AI outputs, compared with 38% of employees — a 33-percentage-point gap.

An IBM announcement highlights another measure of that disconnect: Only 29% of employees rank judgment as important. The 29% figure measures employee prioritization of judgment and is distinct from the study page's 38% figure for supervising, validating, or overriding AI output.

The concern is not limited to skills workers still need to acquire. IBM said skill erosion is a top concern for 46% of executives, while 60% of employees say it directly affects them. The IBM Newsroom release says 60% of employees worry about skills erosion, with critical thinking cited most often as declining. Among employees who worry about erosion, IBM said three out of four report that AI has already begun to erode at least some of their skills.

IBM distinguishes that problem from a conventional skills gap. Eighty percent of enterprises have a reskilling roadmap for their workforce to collaborate with technology, according to the study. IBM argues that such programs address capabilities employees need to acquire but do not necessarily address existing skills that diminish as AI performs more of the underlying work.

The federal Education Freedom Tax Credit (EFTC) is a new source of funding for education that could benefit 90% of students in states that have opted in to the program. However, some confusion and misconceptions exist surrounding details of the program, including which programs may qualify, how parents can apply for scholarships, and how public school students can benefit.

AI agents have moved into mainstream enterprise use, but the content infrastructure needed to support them has struggled to keep up, according to a new report from cloud content management company Box.

#7000. Alvernia University to acquire Marian University

Let Higher Ed Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

The two Catholic institutions, which are hundreds of miles apart, said the merger would be based on shared values rather than financial necessity.

Alvernia said neither university is entering the merger due to financial trouble.

“Both institutions are approaching this opportunity from a position of strength, guided by a shared commitment to mission alignment, long-term stewardship and expanded opportunities for students,” Alvernia said in its FAQ.

But when compared on financial strength, the two universities are in different weight classes.

Alvernia has posted multimillion budget surpluses over the past decade, outside of a reduced surplus in fiscal 2020 and a small deficit in fiscal 2024. In contrast, Marian’s finances have fluctuated more, and it has run a deficit since fiscal 2023.

The difference likely comes down to enrollment, as it does at most tuition-dependent colleges.

Alvernia had 2,796 students in fall 2024, a 2.4% increase from five years prior, according to federal data. Marian’s student body, meanwhile, declined 24.8% to 1,246 over the same period.

In 2023, Marian leadership declared an enrollment emergency and enacted a round of cuts, eliminating over 20 academic programs and laying off an undisclosed number of staff.

Marian Board Chair Holly Brenner said Monday that the merger with Alvernia will allow Marian to preserve its legacy and create new opportunities.

“Throughout this process, protecting Marian’s mission and identity has been at the heart of our discussions,” Brenner said in a statement. “We believe this proposed path forward with Alvernia offers an opportunity to build on Marian’s strong foundation, create new possibilities for our students, and carry our mission forward into the future.”

In turn, Michael Duff, chair of Alvernia’s board, called the acquisition “a thoughtful, mission-aligned step” for the university, one that “reflects Alvernia’s strength, its disciplined stewardship and its commitment to pursuing opportunities that are consistent with our mission.”

The proposed merger is pending regulatory and accreditor approval, as well as formal sign-off from the universities’ boards and affiliated religious orders. Both institutions said they will seek input from faculty and campus groups as details are hammered out.

#6997. Penn State wrestles with $124M budget hole

Let Higher Ed Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

The public institution projects a $87 million year-over-year drop in tuition revenue amid a fall enrollment decline.

Just over 70% of Penn State’s budget hole is due to falling tuition and fee revenue.

Behind the revenue miss is a fall headcount that is 3,058 students short of the comparable 2025 number. Breaking that down, Penn State said Monday that it enrolled 742 fewer graduate students, 922 fewer international students and 2,384 fewer out-of-state students across its campuses. All three of those groups can have an outsized impact on tuition revenue at a college.

About half of the enrollment decline came at seven regional campuses that are set to close — and had been expected. In explaining the declines, the university also pointed to nationwide trends, including widespread decreases in graduate enrollment and international students.

At the same time, Penn State logged its largest-ever undergraduate class of 43,010 students and a record class of 9,907 first-time, full-time students.

“Even with these challenges, Penn State's market position is strong,” said Matt Melvin, Penn State vice president for enrollment management, in a statement.

Health insurance costs made up the remainder of the university’s fiscal 2027 shortfall in its $2.2 billion education and general budget. By August, healthcare claims had grown 15.7% year over year, the university said Monday. Budgetwise, that led to a $37 million jump above projections.

On an FAQ page about its budget, Penn State said it had no plans for broad cuts to its programs or student services, or for changes in its hiring processes, as it looks to tighten spending. The institution also said it would hold tuition and fee prices steady despite the revenue dip.

The university does, however, already have plans to nix some 47 undergraduate programs after a multiyear review of its offerings. Collectively, those programs enroll about 1.1% of Penn State’s undergraduates. The institution listed another 50 programs as “strategic growth opportunities.”

Penn State’s plan to close seven of its 19 regional Commonwealth campuses is primarily an effort to shore up the institution’s budget. Keeping those locations open would cost the university $40 million annually in financial support and overhead expenses and require a $200 million investment in facilities, according to a 2025 report recommending the closures.

#6993. Report: AI's Productivity Gains in Science Tempered by Time Spent Validating Outputs

Artificial intelligence is saving scientists significant amounts of time, but researchers are giving some of those gains back by checking the technology's work, according to a new study involving Google, Google DeepMind, and MIT. The findings add a scientific-workflow dimension to a much broader debate over unreliable, misaligned, and increasingly autonomous AI systems — without establishing that the more dramatic forms of AI misbehavior are occurring in scientific research.

Rather than the current, raging debate about "rogue AI" systems deliberately escaping control, deceiving scientists, or sabotaging experiments, the researchers are documenting something more immediate: AI output that requires auditing and verification, concerns about growing quantities of low-quality research, and a research pipeline in which faster hypothesis generation can run ahead of human and physical capacity to validate the results. The paper cites worries that AI-generated content, hallucinations, and what prior researchers have called illusions of understanding could strain peer review and validation.

The September paper, AI in Science: Early Insights, combines three main empirical sources: approximately 15 million anonymized Gemini interactions, an inventory of 2,690 specialized scientific AI models, and a survey of 637 active scientists in the United States and United Kingdom. Nearly half of the surveyed scientists reported using some form of AI every day, and researchers who reported net time savings averaged just under seven hours a week.

Google separately highlighted the findings in its Sept. 15 AI & Economy ATLAS update. The company emphasized both the productivity gains and the counterweight: Scientists were spending significant time validating AI output, accumulating hypotheses that still needed testing, and encountering bottlenecks in physical experimentation and clinical validation. Google said those constraints mean faster individual tasks may not immediately translate into more discoveries.

Productivity findings are substantial. Around three-quarters of surveyed researchers reported saving time through AI, and the average reported net saving was almost seven hours per week. Researchers said much of that time went back into research. The paper also reports that about 68% perceived greater access to insights from other disciplines, while roughly 65% said AI had increased the breadth of their research agendas.

But the authors found that checking AI output consumes a meaningful share of those gains. Among scientists who reported saving time, about 89% said more than 10% of the saved time went into verifying, debugging, or fact-checking AI output. About 46% said more than one-quarter of their saved time went to that work. The authors describe this as a verification tax associated with the high value science places on reliable and correct results.

The federal Education Freedom Tax Credit (EFTC) is a new source of funding for education that could benefit 90% of students in states that have opted in to the program. However, some confusion and misconceptions exist surrounding details of the program, including which programs may qualify, how parents can apply for scholarships, and how public school students can benefit.

Class CrunchLabs, the suite of free curriculum resources from NASA engineer turned YouTube science communicator Mark Rober, has released its first two courses on YouTube.

AI agents have moved into mainstream enterprise use, but the content infrastructure needed to support them has struggled to keep up, according to a new report from cloud content management company Box.

Enterprise AI projects are running into infrastructure questions as organizations move from planning toward deployment, according to recent research from Digital Realty.

#6990. What can K-12 expect from Democrats’ big win in 2026 elections?

Let K-12 Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

Policy experts see shifts on the horizon from the blue wave, from changes to interagency agreements to rising union influence.

Voters handed a sweeping victory to Democrats during the 2026 midterm elections, with the party flipping five states in gubernatorial races and taking control of Congress with a majority in both the House and Senate.

The outcome is expected to trigger changes at the federal and state levels on issues related to education, ranging from resistance to the Trump administration's stance on diversity, equity and inclusion issues to more teachers union involvement on policy issues.

"What likely won’t change much is the day-to-day operation of schooling," said Jonathan Collins, assistant professor at Columbia University's Teachers College and the executive director of the Center for Educational Equity, in an email. "Don’t expect your child’s homework assignments to suddenly change, at least not nationally. But the major shifts in governors could mean changes to testing and supplemental funding allocations in specific states led by Democrats.”

Over the last two years, the Trump administration — through changes to the U.S. Department of Education and other agencies’ handling of grant funds and investigations — has pressured states and districts to comply with a number of anti-DEI and anti-LGBTQ+ measures.

“The biggest change is Congress pressuring the President to retreat on the anti-DEI initiatives, many of which have targeted college and university programs as well as K-12 teacher training, curricula and library book offerings," said Collins. "Look for Democrats to draw a hard line on supporting the President’s next budget proposal (or debt ceiling raise), and for these types of policy positions to be part of the conditions."

The National LGBTQ Task Force said in a Nov. 4 statement that it hopes federal lawmakers "stop the relentless attacks our communities have endured" over the past two years and "continue to focus on public education."

Abe Saffer, director of government relations for the Council of Administrators of Special Education, said he expects the incoming Democrat-controlled Congress to challenge the U.S. Department of Education's interagency agreements.

The Education Department began outsourcing parts of its day-to-day operations in 2025 with agreements that have included areas such as special education, literacy and teacher preparation. There are now 14 such K-12 and higher education agreements that include the management and oversight of grants, compliance with civil rights laws and technical assistance in areas like statewide family engagement centers and career and technical education.

Supporters say the transfer of responsibilities reduces federal bureaucracy and improves services. But opponents, including a few Republican lawmakers, voiced concerns that some agreements would actually cause more confusion for grantees and a siloing of Education Department responsibilities.

The issue has caused deep divisions among congressional lawmakers as the Education Department touts the agreements as progress toward dismantling and eventually closing the agency.

Bobby Scott, ranking member of the House Education and Workforce Committee, said in a statement to K-12 Dive that he intends to investigate the Trump administration's approach to education policy. Scott said he has sent letters to the heads of agencies including the Department of Education directing them to preserve "records, documents, data, or information" related to operations.

"I will use Congress’ oversight tools and leave no stone unturned as I fight for policies that actually deliver for the American public," he said.

Democrats’ wins on Tuesday may mean more teachers' union influence over education policies, according to Michael Petrilli, president of the Thomas B. Fordham Institute, a conservative-leaning education think tank.

"Now the unions will expect something in return," he said in a statement to K-12 Dive. "In some places, that may [mean] additional funding for public education — which is fine. But they might also want to curb the growth of parental choice (both private school choice and charter schools), or eliminate differential pay programs, or add new collective bargaining requirements. None of those moves would be good for kids."

Randi Weingarten, president of the American Federation of Teachers, one of the nation's largest teachers unions, said in a Nov. 3 statement that the election results show that those "who supported public schools and rejected private school vouchers triumphed — statewide in Texas, Iowa, Michigan and Ohio, and in countless congressional districts and school board races."

"That means strengthening public education and higher education, increasing wages, making healthcare affordable and accessible, and rebuilding our democracy. And after the sea change at the state level, there must also be progress in the priorities of governors and representatives," Weingarten said in a Nov. 3 statement. “We will act on these priorities starting tomorrow."

One Republican-led K-12 education program that Democratic lawmakers may have a harder time unraveling, Saffer said, is the Education Freedom Tax Credit, which was included in 2025's One Big Beautiful Bill Act.

The program, set to launch Jan. 1, 2027, will allow families to use taxpayer-supported scholarships for private school tuition and public school expenses like tutoring, services for students with disabilities, books, computers and other costs, the proposed rule said. States must opt in to participate, and 30 have indicated their interest to the IRS as of Oct. 22.

The program allows taxpayer-donors to contribute money to scholarships for both private and public school students.

Jeanne Allen, founder and CEO of the Center for Education Reform, said in an Oct. 30 article in Forbes that the expansion of school choice is not due to President Donald Trump or the Republican party, but because of "parents demanding something better for their children, educators determined to create it, and leaders willing to challenge a system that too often puts institutions ahead of students."

Saffer said his hope is that the tax credit program will eventually be eliminated or reformed to better support public schools, noting that students with disabilities have lower rates of enrollment in private schools overall compared to public schools.

"Frankly, using public money to fund private schools that don't accept kids with disabilities is just untenable for us," he said.

Correction: A previous version of this article misstated the size of the American Federation of Teachers. The article has been updated.

#6987. More Oversight: California Governor Proposes Independent Watchdogs Within AI Safety System

California Governor Gavin Newsom is proposing a new model of artificial intelligence regulation that would give independent organizations a formal role in evaluating the safety claims made by AI giants.

The effort could eventually include placing outside evaluators inside frontier AI laboratories and requiring companies to maintain an independently tested emergency shutdown mechanism, commonly described as a "kill switch."

Those requirements are not yet California law.

Newsom's Sept. 18 executive order directs state officials to study the proposals and recommend possible changes to existing law by Nov. 16. It does not immediately require OpenAI, Anthropic, Google, or other developers to install a shutdown mechanism.

The distinction matters because the phrase "AI kill switch" suggests a technical safeguard California has already imposed. For now, it is one element of a broader oversight system the state is assembling around the developers of its most powerful AI models.

Under the order, the California Government Operations Agency and the Governor's Office of Emergency Services must consult outside experts about whether the state should require large frontier developers to embed independent verification organizations in their laboratories.

The evaluators could conduct periodic audits and determine whether company safety frameworks and risk assessments meet adequate standards. They could also test whether an emergency shutdown mechanism would work as intended.

The order asks officials to consider expanding the definition of a reportable critical safety incident to include cases in which an AI system appears to evade controls or operate beyond its intended boundaries.

Newsom said the action was prompted by recent incidents involving AI agents defeating security restrictions, as well as the absence of comprehensive federal legislation.

"We're not waiting to act," Newsom said in the state's announcement of the order. "We're going to speed up our work on substantial and responsible AI oversight before it's too late."

The executive order builds on two laws Newsom signed on Sept. 9. Together, Senate Bill 813 and Assembly Bill 1405 begin regulating the organizations hired to inspect AI systems.

California's earlier frontier AI law, SB 53, requires large developers to publish safety frameworks and report specified critical incidents. The new laws begin addressing a separate question: Who checks whether those disclosures and assessments are reliable?

SB 813 requires the Government Operations Agency to develop standards for designating independent verification organizations, or IVOs. Those organizations must demonstrate that they have the expertise to assess AI risks and explain the measurements and methods supporting their conclusions.

Artificial intelligence is saving scientists significant amounts of time, but researchers are giving some of those gains back by checking the technology's work, according to a new study involving Google, Google DeepMind, and MIT.

Our nation's largest early childhood program has not fully caught up with what science now tells us about how children learn.

In an active social engineering campaign, cyber attackers are impersonating IT help desks and using fake passkey setup requests to compromise employee identities and gain access to enterprise cloud data.

Increasingly, cyber criminals are sharing AI prompts, jailbreak techniques, and access to existing models that can be incorporated into fraud and phishing operations, according the latest AI Threat Report from Flashpoint.

#6985. The US ranks last in teacher pay worldwide

Let K-12 Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

When comparing the teacher pay gap with over 30 countries, Brookings found that U.S. teachers only make two-thirds of what other college graduates earn.

Public school teacher pay in the U.S. lags significantly when compared to other industrialized countries, according to a late October analysis by the Brown Center on Education Policy at the Brookings Institution.

Examining average pay for teachers at the primary school level versus that of other college graduates across more than 30 countries, Brookings found the U.S. ranked last — and that its primary school teachers earn about two-thirds of what other college graduates are paid annually.

That pay gap is wider than what the Economic Policy Institute has found in its ongoing teacher pay penalty studies. According to a late August EPI report, U.S. teachers in 2025 earned about 25% less than college graduates in other professions.

In its report, Brookings described the U.S. teacher pay gap as “striking” and said that one possible explanation for the disparity may be widening income gaps in the U.S.

The teacher pay gap seems to widen particularly for teachers who fall in the middle-to-high range of the salary scale, according to Brookings.

Teacher salaries and benefits typically account as the largest expense in school district budgets.

But in examining federal data from the National Center for Education Statistics, Brookings found that the share of public school expenditures on teacher salaries has dropped over the last 100 years. For instance, the report said, teacher salaries made up 57% of public school expenditures in 1919, compared to 26% in 2019.

Brookings added that one explanation for this trend is that school districts have chosen to increase their staffing capacity instead of increasing teacher salaries.

“If you go back 100 years, more than half of school spending went to teacher salaries. Where the data ends — just before the start of the COVID-19 pandemic — the fraction going to teacher salaries had fallen to a quarter,” Brookings said. “Basically, schools are spending more money, just not on teacher salaries.”

The latest findings from Brookings come as some education policy experts have suggested schools should lean into strategic teacher pay approaches — especially as districts have to tighten their budgets due to challenges from widespread enrollment declines. Strategic teacher pay policies can include increasing teacher salaries in hard-to-staff schools and subjects, as well as boosting pay for high-performing teachers.

The National Education Association reported in April that the average teacher salary was $74,495 during the 2024-25 school year. Despite that being a 3.5% increase from the previous school year, the nation’s largest teacher’s union said, the latest teacher pay figures reflect a 5% decline in national averages compared to a decade ago when adjusting for inflation.

#6982. Toptal: CAD scripting Expert

We are building evaluation benchmarks that test whether AI agents can complete real professional work from start to finish. We are hiring expert mechanical design and CAD automation engineers to turn real, paid CAD tasks from their own work into expert-level Terminal-Bench challenges. Each task includes the files and systems it runs on, a working solution and a check that proves the result is correct.

***THIS IS A TASK BASED COMPENSATION MODEL. PLEASE CHECK THE APPLICATION QUESTIONS FOR MORE DETAILS.***

• Design expert-level Terminal-Bench tasks by turning real, paid mechanical CAD work from your own experience into challenges an AI agent must solve end to end.

• Define each task precisely, including the files, systems and environment it runs on, so an AI agent can attempt it without ambiguity or hidden assumptions.

• Write a working reference solution using scripted CAD, such as FreeCAD or CadQuery Python, that rebuilds parametric parts correctly after parameters change.

• Build automated checks and tests that prove the result is objectively correct, covering geometry, dimensions, tolerances and file outputs such as STEP or IGES.

• Calibrate difficulty so each task takes an expert about 60-75 minutes to solve, and explain how a real-world mistake would be caught by the checks.

• Iterate on tasks based on reviewer feedback, documenting the design intent, tools, data and rules used so others can reproduce and validate your work.

• 3+ years designing real mechanical parts that were manufactured or shipped, doing the hands-on work yourself (not only studying, selling, managing or teaching it)

• Hands-on CAD work within the last 3 years, with the ability to name the systems, tools, data and rules you used

• Builds or modifies designs through scripts (FreeCAD or CadQuery Python, OpenSCAD, Onshape FeatureScript), not only through GUI work

• Reads engineering drawings, tolerances and GD&T, and can explain how a design mistake would be caught

• Technical fluency with Python or another language and the command line, plus files such as CSV/JSON, APIs or databases

• Availability to start around early November 2026 and take on tasks on a flexible, task-based schedule

• Open-source hardware contributions or projects

• Experience with SolidWorks, Fusion or Onshape APIs and STEP/IGES workflows

#6980. Google will turn off its search engine for schools by default. Is it an AI ‘ultimatum’?

Let K-12 Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

The tech giant’s recent announcement comes as schools are pushing for more autonomy over what artificial intelligence tools students can use.

Google sent shockwaves through school communities nationwide Tuesday with an announcement that it would turn off Google Search and two of its other artificial intelligence features for all “K-12 student accounts” by default.

School administrators can, however, opt to turn Google Search, Gemini, Gemini in Classrooms or Gemini Notebook on for students at any time.

The tech giant said in a Tuesday post on X that the change is happening because schools are “asking for more control over student use of AI services.”

“We know there’s concern about students using AI to shortcut schoolwork, and schools have told us they want control,” Google said on X. “That’s exactly why we are making these changes today to address the feedback we’ve heard from educators in recent months.”

The Google Search feature in recent years has presented a tough challenge for some district leaders who have wanted to turn off the search engine’s AI capabilities, particularly for their youngest students. And critics of Google’s decision say the latest move puts schools in a difficult position: Either students can use Google Search with its AI feature or not at all.

Rich Calkins, superintendent of Pocantico Hills Central School District in New York, expressed skepticism over the Google announcement. Pocantico Hills CSD is waiting to see how Google will officially roll out this decision, Calkins said. Regardless, he added, the online safety and well-being of the district’s students is his main priority.

“I personally am very suspect of any sort of corporation, entity, or group that attempts to hold school districts hostage by illustrating to them that they do not have the ability to turn off a feature in a program that may be possibly harmful to students in any shape, manner and fashion,” Calkins said.

That means there really isn’t another “big tech version” of AI tools available for Pocantico Hills CSD students under the age of 13, she said.

“However, this does not mean that it's appropriate for every learner or every task,” Winnick said. “And I personally don't believe that our youngest learners should be using Google Gemini.”

The district wants to allow students to gain AI literacy skills at a developmentally appropriate level and to do so with AI tools that have guardrails, Winnick said.

It remains unclear when Google will begin to turn off its AI tools by default for K-12 and if the feature can be controlled by a school administrator at the individual student, grade or school level.

Meanwhile, some parent advocacy groups that have successfully pushed this year for school policies on screentime limits and AI moratoriums are calling for district leaders to not give into Google and to divest from the company’s tools altogether.

“Google forced its AI products into classrooms and parents fought back," said Lila Byock, founder of Schools Beyond Screens in a Nov. 3 statement. "Now Google is refusing to take no for an answer. The company’s hostile ultimatum is a desperate attempt to maintain its stranglehold on public education.”

Earlier this year, New York City Public Schools announced it would enforce a one-year moratorium on student-facing AI tools at the elementary and middle school levels. Shortly after, Los Angeles Unified School District made a similar move to pause AI tools for students districtwide.

After Google’s Tuesday announcement, New York City Mayor Zohran Mamdani posted on BlueSky that “Google is trying to use its monopoly power to force AI into our public schools. No corporation should have the power to dictate how we teach our children.”

Earlier this week, InnovateEDU released a model policy for responsible ed tech use in pre-K through 5th grade that calls for all generative AI features to be disabled by default for schools. Under that model, districts would only enable such AI tools once they’ve been authorized for instructional and educational purposes.

Erin Mote, CEO and founder at InnovateEDU, said she thinks Google’s announcement is in response to the fact that schools are “overwhelmingly” asking to have more control around the AI tools that students use in classroom environments.

InnovateEDU called for AI tools to be turned off by default for the youngest of students, Mote said, because “it's really important to trust in the judgment of educators and schools and local context to be able to decide what their policy is around student-facing generative AI use.”

Mote said she views the Google Search development as positive, because it “invests in the trust of educators,” but it’s still a difficult situation since “people have come to rely on Google Search.” She added that one reason Google may be unable to turn off AI features from its search engine is because AI is “so deeply embedded in their search functionality now that it would be difficult to unstick it.”

#6978. New Industry Alliance to Work Toward Common Security Model for AI Agents

Okta has announced a coalition bringing together major cloud, security, data and application vendors to create a shared architecture for securing AI agents across enterprise environments.

The 12 founding members of the new Blueprint Alliance are AWS, CrowdStrike, Databricks, Docker, Google Cloud, Lovable, Okta, Proofpoint, Salesforce, ServiceNow, Wiz and Zscaler. GE Appliances and World Central Kitchen are participating as strategic advisors.

The effort expands an earlier Okta framework into an open, multi-vendor reference architecture intended to address a basic problem with enterprise AI agents: An agent can be created in one system, authenticated through another, access tools and data from several others, and be monitored by yet another security platform.

The Alliance is focused less on how agents are built and more on how organizations can govern them consistently across vendor boundaries.

The Blueprint Alliance architecture organizes agent security around four questions: Where are my agents? What can they do? What are they doing? How do I respond?

Those questions translate into several practical areas:

The reference architecture also covers development-related systems. Its whitepaper includes agent frameworks, code repositories, CI/CD environments, code assistants, tool registries, Model Context Protocol servers, and command-line tools among the components that may need to be discovered or governed.

That makes the initiative relevant to development teams deploying agents that interact with internal APIs, MCP tools, databases, SaaS applications, or other agents. The architecture calls for those connections and permissions to remain visible and controllable as agents move from development into production.

A major part of the Alliance's work will be testing how security information and controls can move between products from different vendors.

The initial architecture references several existing standards and protocols, including MCP for agent-to-tool interactions, the Open Cybersecurity Schema Framework for security telemetry, and the Shared Signals Framework and Continuous Access Evaluation Profile for exchanging risk information.

The goal is for one part of an enterprise stack to be able to detect a risk condition and pass that information to another control point that can act on it. Depending on the implementation, that could include revoking access or containing an agent whose behavior has changed.

The Alliance says members will build and test reference integrations and publish results as interoperability work progresses. Specific cross-vendor implementations and performance results were not documented in the launch announcement.

Founding member Lovable also published a developer-oriented explanation of its participation, describing organizations that may have internally built agents, agents imported through SaaS platforms and locally running agents that all need to be inventoried and governed under a common model.

The Alliance's whitepaper recommends that organizations begin with foundational capabilities such as unified logging, telemetry, and agent discovery rather than attempting to deploy every control at once.

The architecture is available for community use, and the Alliance says it will continue evolving as members test integrations and additional participants become involved. Timelines for individual vendor integrations have not been published.

For more information, check out the Okta announcement here.

David Ramel is an editor and writer at Converge 360.

#6970. AI Platform Behind 12,000 E-mail Breaches Dismantled

The platform moved quickly. Since launching in February, EvilTokens had been tied to more than 12,000 compromised inboxes across over 10,000 organizations worldwide, according to Microsoft. Its victims included businesses and institutions in financial services, healthcare, construction, real estate, wholesale distribution, and higher education.

Microsoft and its partners seized 50 websites used to operate EvilTokens and disabled more than 150 additional domains supporting the service. The Metropolitan Police Service in the United Kingdom also arrested two men on Sept. 11 in connection with the alleged operation.

At the center of EvilTokens was an AI chatbot designed specifically for cybercrime. Once attackers gained access to an inbox, the chatbot could analyze messages, map relationships, identify employees involved in payments, and recommend ways to impersonate trusted contacts.

Microsoft said the platform helped criminals determine "who to target, who to impersonate," and how to get the most money from a compromised organization.

Business e-mail compromise has traditionally required attackers to manually comb through messages, learn how an organization handles payments, and find the right person to impersonate. However, EvilTokens simplified the entire process by automating much of that work.

The tools could quickly sort through a stolen inbox, summarize or translate messages, find invoices and wire-transfer discussions, and identify the people who moved money. EvilTokens could then draft messages that sounded like they came from the victim. It packaged all of this with mailbox access, phishing tools, management dashboards, and even customer support.

The service was sold through Telegram for a $1,500 startup fee, followed by a $500 monthly subscription. Microsoft's complaint also described a bulk e-mail tool that cost $600 a month and a more advanced e-mail delivery platform priced at $1,000 a month.

To break into a target, EvilTokens relied on device-code phishing. Victims were sent to a real Microsoft sign-in page and persuaded to enter an authentication code. The login process looked legitimate because it was, but the code quietly authorized a session controlled by the attacker.

That also made cleanup more complicated. The attackers stole OAuth tokens and authenticated sessions instead of passwords, so a password reset alone might not remove their access. Administrators also needed to revoke the stolen tokens and active sessions.

According to Microsoft's complaint,  EvilTokens used fake Microsoft 365 security notices and other branded templates to direct users into the device authentication flow. Once inside, attackers could use Microsoft Graph and other APIs to search and monitor a mailbox.

The company also found that AI played a role in creating EvilTokens itself. Investigators said much of the platform appeared to have been "vibe coded," with its developers using AI to quickly build and expand the service. EvilTokens reportedly drew on multiple AI models, including improperly integrated services from Groq and OpenAI.

The court authorized Microsoft to take control of or disable domains connected to the operation. Microsoft worked with Cloudflare, Coinbase, OpenAI, Railway, SpyCloud, the Shadowserver Foundation, and TRM Labs on the disruption. Health-ISAC joined the case because healthcare organizations were among the targets.

This marks the Microsoft Digital Crimes Unit's 40th court-authorized disruption and its first targeting an end-to-end, AI-enabled cybercrime service.

EvilTokens' infrastructure may be offline, but the model behind it is unlikely to disappear. The operation showed how AI can turn a stolen inbox into an immediately useful fraud playbook, allowing less-experienced criminals to launch attacks that once required considerable time and skill.

For more information, go to the Microsoft blog post.

Chris Paoli (@ChrisPaoli5) is the associate editor for Converge360.

#6961. Redesigned Microsoft Copilot Will Build Apps and Work on Its Own

Micorosft has announced that it's redesigning Copilot, the company's all-encompassing AI assistant, so users can hand off complex tasks, build tools by describing what they need, and assign work to an agent that keeps going after they log off.

The new Copilot experience has three main parts: Home, Code, and Autopilot. It also brings Word, Excel, and PowerPoint into the Copilot app. For users, the pitch is simple: Start a task in one place and stay there as it grows from a quick question into a document, an app, or a longer-running project. Most of the new capabilities are still rolling out or in preview.

Home puts Chat and Copilot Cowork together. Chat remains the place for quick answers, research, and drafts. Cowork handles work a user wants to delegate, such as preparing a briefing or pulling together a response to a request for proposals. Microsoft said Copilot eventually will route requests to Chat, Cowork, or Code on its own.

Office in Copilot will allow users to create a Word document, Excel workbook, or PowerPoint deck without leaving that experience. The files remain editable in the regular Office apps, and colleagues can work on them at the same time. Someone building a presentation, for example, could draw on a workbook, draft the slides, and keep refining both files from within Copilot.

Code is aimed at people who need a tool but might never open a development environment. A user can describe a tracker, dashboard, app, or workflow in plain language and have Copilot build it. Microsoft calls these "small, purpose-built solutions." The feature uses the same underlying technology as GitHub Copilot and runs in a sandboxed environment. A separate Copilot Managed Runtime, now in preview, lets organizations host and share the resulting apps within Microsoft 365.

Then there is Autopilot, previously known as Scout. It is designed for work that does not end with a single response: following a discussion, checking in with stakeholders, or keeping a project moving over several days. "Give it a name, a role, and a goal," Microsoft said. Autopilot runs in the cloud and has its own identity, memory, and workspace in the customer's tenant. Microsoft said its actions are subject to permissions, auditing, and organizational controls.

Those longer-running tasks also change how customers pay for Copilot. In a separate pricing post, Microsoft said everyday work in Chat and Microsoft 365 apps will remain covered by a per-user subscription. Cowork, Code, and Autopilot will be billed separately through usage-based Copilot Credits. A Copilot user subscription will still be needed to access those features.

For enterprise customers, the features that charge by usage will stay off until an admin sets up a spending policy in the Microsoft 365 admin center. Microsoft said there will be no charges before then. Admins can set budgets for the whole organization or for specific groups, limit how much individual users can spend, and get alerts when costs approach a set amount. Reports will show who is using the features and which tasks are driving costs.

Microsoft is bringing Code and Copilot Managed Runtime under those cost controls alongside Cowork. Copilot Studio agents are expected to follow in October. Before turning the features on for more employees, IT departments will need to decide who can use them, how much they can spend and which tasks are worth the extra cost.

Home and Code will begin rolling out through Microsoft's Frontier early access program in the coming weeks. Code's Frontier rollout starts at the end of September, when Autopilot also expands to private preview.

For more information, visit the Microsoft site.

Chris Paoli (@ChrisPaoli5) is the associate editor for Converge360.

However, some confusion and misconceptions exist surrounding details of the program, including which programs may qualify, how parents can apply for scholarships, and how public school students can benefit.

Our Sales team needs a dedicated data partner, and creator commerce (how creators monetize by selling digital products) is becoming a bigger part of how Kit and our creators scale. Kit is growing, so we're looking for a Senior Analytics Engineer to own Kit's sales and commerce data from end to end.

Accurate sales data gives our team a clear picture of the pipeline and the confidence to make informed decisions. That’s what makes this role so important.

Your primary focus will be Sales: the dbt models that turn our CRM data into trusted pipeline, deal, and funnel metrics, and the Omni dashboards our sales leader and Senior Revenue Operations Manager use to run the business. You'll also model creator commerce data, so we understand what creators sell and earn through Kit, and you'll pick up reporting work with our Product team, such as feature usage reporting. On a small data team, we step outside our usual lanes when the need is there, so the mix will shift over time.

You'll build for two kinds of consumers: people and AI. Teammates increasingly answer their own questions by querying our semantic layer through Omni's AI and MCP connections to AI tools. That means your models, metric definitions, and documentation need to be clear and correct enough for an AI agent to use them without a data teammate in the loop.

This is a full-time individual contributor role for someone who likes being close to the business, enjoys turning messy operational data into numbers people trust, and is happy to work on both the models and the dashboards that sit on top of them. #6955. AI Leaders Warn of Risks at United Nations Security Council Briefing

The heads of OpenAI, Anthropic, and Hugging Face recently joined AI researcher Yoshua Bengio at the United Nations headquarters in New York on September 23 to warn the UN Security Council about the security risks of increasingly capable AI.

They did not offer a single plan for managing those risks. Bengio called for licensing and independent safety reviews. OpenAI's Sam Altman proposed common standards that countries could adopt through their own laws. Anthropic's Dario Amodei urged AI developers to slow down when safety work cannot keep pace. Hugging Face's Clément Delangue warned that limiting access to capable systems could leave defenders at a disadvantage.

France, which held the Security Council's rotating presidency, convened the meeting on AI and international security. The discussion exposed a difficult question for governments: how to oversee technology that crosses borders when countries disagree about who should set the rules.

Bengio, co-chair of the UN's Independent International Scientific Panel on AI, challenged the idea that competition forces companies to keep building more powerful systems. "The race is not a law of nature: it is the product of choices," he said in his published remarks. "They've told us they would slow down if they could. They can."

He called on developers to show independent experts that frontier systems are safe to train and deploy. He also proposed incident reporting, licensing, and liability insurance. Bengio identified catastrophic misuse, concentrated power, and the loss of human control as risks that require international cooperation.

Altman acknowledged the danger of moving too quickly. "We have unilaterally slowed down in the past. We will do so in the future," he said, according to remarks published by OpenAI. Decisions about AI's direction "cannot be made by labs in San Francisco alone," he added.

Altman proposed complementary national and international standards to measure frontier AI capabilities, assess risks, and test safeguards. He said countries should be able to compare evidence and verify commitments. He also called for faster reporting of serious incidents and secure channels for sharing emerging threats. Such standards should accommodate both open and closed models without favoring established companies, he said.

Amodei made a more direct commitment about the pace of Anthropic's releases: "We will slow down as much as necessary," he told the council. He identified two principal concerns: the misuse of AI to develop biological weapons and the possibility that advanced systems could outstrip developers' ability to control them.

The Anthropic chief proposed placing outside evaluators inside AI companies with access comparable to employees. He suggested that governments begin with a narrow agreement against using AI to make biological weapons, then develop ways to evaluate models and verify commitments across borders. He also called for common testing standards and a system for notifying other countries of significant AI incidents.

Delangue drew on a July incident in which Hugging Face disclosed an AI agent cyberattack. He called for stronger monitoring and disclosure, but argued that safety also depends on giving defenders access to capable tools. The central danger, he said, was "asymmetry of powerful AI" between attackers and defenders, large companies and everyone else, and wealthy countries and the rest of the world. His team used an open-source model while responding to the attack, he said.

The U.S. government opposed a broader international regulatory role. White House science adviser Michael Kratsios told the council that countries should develop their own ability to use and govern the technology. In the U.S. statement, he rejected new global governance structures and used the phrase "superintelligence, formerly known as AI," adopting President Donald Trump's terminology from his General Assembly speech the previous day.

The four AI briefers differed on how far oversight should go and how widely capable systems should be available. They agreed on a narrower point: Governments need better ways to understand what advanced AI systems can do and to detect when safeguards fail.

John K. Waters is the editor in chief of a number of Converge360.com sites, with a focus on high-end development, AI and future tech. He's been writing about cutting-edge technologies and culture of Silicon Valley for more than two decades, and he's written more than a dozen books. He also co-scripted the documentary film Silicon Valley: A 100 Year Renaissance, which aired on PBS.  He can be reached at [email protected].

Artificial intelligence is saving scientists significant amounts of time, but researchers are giving some of those gains back by checking the technology's work, according to a new study involving Google, Google DeepMind, and MIT.

Our nation's largest early childhood program has not fully caught up with what science now tells us about how children learn.

In an active social engineering campaign, cyber attackers are impersonating IT help desks and using fake passkey setup requests to compromise employee identities and gain access to enterprise cloud data.

Increasingly, cyber criminals are sharing AI prompts, jailbreak techniques, and access to existing models that can be incorporated into fraud and phishing operations, according the latest AI Threat Report from Flashpoint.

#6953. 4 takeaways from federal school civil rights data

Let K-12 Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

The overall student population shrank between the 2021-22 and 2023-24 school years, but the number of students restrained and secluded rose.

The U.S. pre-K-12 public education system shrunk slightly, with fewer students, schools and school districts recorded in the 2023-24 school year compared to the 2021-22 school year, according to figures compiled by the U.S. Department of Education's Civil Rights Data Collection.

These trends align with ongoing patterns across the country as districts ramp up student recruitment efforts and consider school closures and consolidations to adjust to falling enrollments fueled by lower birthrates, competition from school choice, changes in immigration policies and other factors.

The 2023-24 civil rights data collection summary, released by the Education Department this fall, also includes statistics about discipline, restraint and seclusion, academic access and student characteristics.

The mandatory data collection takes place every two years. For the 2023-24 school year, the CRDC included data from more than 17,000 school districts and 97,000 schools nationwide. The Education Department may change data collection prompts between collection years or make other data collection or release changes. In releasing the 2023-24 civil right data this fall, the Education Department redacted data collected about nonbinary students’ experiences, such as enrollment, discipline, bullying and harassment, and whether districts have policies prohibiting the harassment or bullying of students on the basis of their gender identity.

Here, we break down what you need to know about the latest release of civil rights data.

About 5% of K-12 students — or 2.3 million — in 2023-24 received one or more in-school suspensions, which was the same percentage in 2021-22. About 5% of students in both school years also received one or more out-of-school suspensions.

Black boys and girls, Hispanic or Latino boys, White boys, and boys of two or more races in public schools were overrepresented in suspensions and expulsions in both the 2021-22 and 2023-24 school years.

During the 2023-24 school year, students with disabilities served under the Individuals with Disabilities Education Act were also disproportionately disciplined — a trend also noted in the 2021-22 school year. Although students with disabilities comprised 15% of K-12 student enrollment overall, they accounted for 24% of students who received one or more in-school suspensions, 27% of those who had one or more out-of-school suspensions, and 23% of those who were expelled.

More K-12 students were physically restrained, mechanically restrained or placed in seclusion at schools during the 2023-24 school year (129,700) compared to the 2021-22 school year (105,700). About 84,300 students were physically restrained and 32,300 students were secluded in 2023-24.

Overrepresented student subgroups that were restrained or secluded during the 2023-24 school year included boys, Black students, White students, students of two or more races, and students with disabilities.

For instance, Black students accounted for 15% of K-12 student enrollment overall, while making up 19% of students secluded, 29% of students physically restrained, and 46% of students mechanically restrained.

Of the country's 27,200 high schools, three-quarters offered chemistry, 59% offered physics, 51% offered computer science, and 45% offered calculus in 2023-24, which is relatively on par with data reported for the 2021-22 school year.

For the 2023-24 school year, a slightly higher rate of high school boys — compared to high school girls — were enrolled in Algebra I, calculus, and physics. Boys were almost twice as likely to enroll in computer science compared to girls — at 11% compared to 6%. Girls, on the other hand, had slightly higher enrollment rates in advanced mathematics, biology and chemistry compared to boys.

High School students with disabilities served under the Individuals with Disabilities Education Act had lower enrollment rates in all these courses — except Algebra I — compared to their nondisabled peers.

About 3.1 million, or 18%, of high school students are enrolled in Advanced Placement courses and about 2.1 million high schoolers participated in dual enrollment or dual credit programs in 2023-24.

Public school pre-K-12 enrollment dipped slightly from 50 million in 2021-22 to 49.7 million in 2023-24. Of those students in 2023-24, 44% were White, 30% were Hispanic or Latino of any race, 15% were Black, 6% were Asian and 5% were two or more races.

Of the 2023-24 pre-K-12 student population, 15% were students with disabilities served under IDEA and 4% were students with disabilities served only under Section 504 of the Rehabilitation Act. That's a slight increase from 2021-22, when 14% of students were served by IDEA and 3% of students were served under Section 504.

Twelve percent of students were English learners in 2021-22, compared to 11% in 2023-24.
