#6879. Unraveling All the Details Behind Wicker’s Wicker

We asked the artists behind Alexander Skarsgård’s woven-to-order suit how they built it—and kept his appeal intact.

“It will take her another lifetime to understand wicker,” Marella Agnelli reportedly said of fellow socialite Mercedes Bass’s apartment. With Alexander Skarsgård’s help, some of us are getting there a little faster.

In Wicker, the romantic comedy based on Ursula Wills’s short story “The Wicker Husband,” Olivia Colman plays a fisherwoman who commissions a husband from the local basketmaker (Peter Dinklage). Skarsgård plays her woven-to-order spouse, whose resemblance to patio furniture does surprisingly little to diminish his sex appeal. Directed by Eleanor Wilson and Alex Huston Fischer, the film comes out in theaters today.

Behind the wicker transformation is Wētā Workshop, the New Zealand design and effects company whose credits include The Lord of the Rings and Avatar. Its artists make the costumes, props, and creatures that give imaginary worlds something tangible—and, in this case, marriageable.

For prosthetic makeup designer Joe Dunckley and lead makeup effects artist Sarah Rubano, the assignment was to turn Skarsgård into a convincing wicker husband without losing the expressions that make him recognizable. The team spent 18 months developing and manufacturing his suit and facial prosthetics, translating his features into a weave, right down to his furrowed brows.

Naturally, we had questions. How do you make wicker sexy? What happens to the eyelashes at lunch? And could anyone involved look at a household basket the same way again? Below, Dunckley and Rubano explain the work behind the wicker.

Before we get to the cheekbones, how did this assignment arrive?

Dunckley: We were approached by Eleanor Wilson and Alex Huston Fischer about 18 months out from filming. Initially, they were after a piece of concept art—a beautiful piece that they could take to potential cast members. This was part of their development process, so they had a tool for helping show their vision for the project.

Pretty quickly after that came the question: How are you going to make this wicker husband character? So much of the film’s success hinges on the success of that character. They always wanted to make the wicker husband a physical prosthetic character. That led us into a process of research and development. It was an 18-month period of R&D and manufacture to get to the final product.

A husband made to order. What, exactly, was in the order?

Dunckley: About 95% of the prosthetic and fabricated suit is made out of a 3D-printed material. Most prosthetic materials are manufactured to imitate human skin, but the wicker husband is built of wicker, not flesh and bones, so we knew we needed something different.

Sometimes we found the limits of existing programs and had to write our own code for weaving. Weaving is such complex geometry that it was very challenging for sculptors to weave in 3D.

The prosthetic appliances were then printed directly, without needing to go through the mold-making process.

Rubano: On [Skarsgård’s] body, we had about 50 individual pieces stitched and anchored onto thin spandex or mesh. The face, including the wig, was about 10 individual pieces. We also used foam latex for the lips, eye bags, and underlip and chin piece.

Did anyone get to weave an actual basket, or was this strictly a digital operation?

Dunckley: Absolutely. We had a team of weavers who had just come off Avatar, where they’d been weaving costumes for the Na’vi. Our design team would block out the shapes that we liked, and our lead weaver would do a physical weave sample using flax, giving us those shapes.

We’d print, do a makeup test, and learn what we learned: This one’s too loose. This one’s too tight and it binds. We’ll need more movement here. Slowly, we developed what you ended up seeing in the film.

I had a crush on the wicker husband. How do you make wicker handsome?

Dunckley: One of our main objectives was to make sure Alexander shines through the makeup. He’s a handsome man, and he has an expressive quality that’s really unique and recognizable.

We wanted to get those qualities into the weave. It was a matter of deconstructing what it is about those expressions that are endearing, that are heart-melting, and then reconstructing them in a weave pattern and enabling that movement for him through the prosthetic.

How long did it take for Alexander Skarsgård to become the wicker husband every day of filming?

Rubano: We got the whole morning process down to an hour and a half. That was the brief at the beginning, because he was working every day. It was an ambitious shooting schedule, so all the build techniques supported being able to expedite it.

He would come in, and we would put on a partial fabricated undersuit. He would come to the makeup chair, then go back and get more of the body suit on. Then we got into gluing the appliances to his face, the eyelashes, the wig, the styling, the makeup around the eyes—all the finishing touches.

And at lunch? Surely even a wicker husband gets a break.

Rubano: About halfway through, once we were really confident with our processes, we started giving him a bit of relief at lunchtime. I would take off the eyelashes because they’re quite robust, and he’s not used to wearing fake eyelashes. We would wrap up his beard and put a little bib over it so he could eat and be comfortable. We’d take off the gloves—just all the things we could do to give him some reprieve. After lunch, we’d build him up 100% again. The cleanup at the end of the day was about half an hour. The real challenge was keeping him cool. He runs hot, so we had fans and ice packs.

How do you feel about all the online chatter about “sexy wicker”?

Dunckley: We never would have expected this kind of level, to be honest. It’s been quite a ride to watch the enthusiasm, all the excitement, all the memes. We sat around watching memes for hours and laughing our heads off.

Rubano: We’ve built so many different types of characters for so many different types of films over 30-plus years. Sometimes they strike a chord as hoped, sometimes they don’t. Sometimes the chord has been struck louder than you could ever have expected. This is an example of that.

Has this changed how you look at the wicker in your own home?

Rubano: I have this tiny little wicker basket, and it’s not necessarily sexy, but I do love it. I look at it with love and adoration. It’s just this tiny little basket I put all my jewelry bits and pieces in. But, of course, the hottest basket of all is Alexander Skarsgård. Definitely.

Julia Cancilla is the social media & news editor at ELLE Decor, where she oversees the brand's socials and covers design, pop culture, and emerging trends. She also authors the monthly ELLE Decoroscope column. Her work has appeared in Inked magazine, House Beautiful, Marie Claire, and more.

#6878. 8 Angelic Powdery Perfumes That Smell Like Heaven on Earth

These aren’t your grandmother's powder perfumes, FYI.

We may earn commission from links on this page, but we only recommend products we love. Promise.

Once upon a time, powdery perfumes only smelled like powder cosmetic compacts with overwhelming hints of violet, orris, rose, and musk. The original powder-forward scents probably reminded you of your grandmother's lipstick or her signature floral scent (no offense, Nana). The classic fragrance family has gotten a bit of a makeover, with a whole new vibe that’s fluffy, heavenly, and fit for any age.

Today's powdery perfumes are complex, thanks to the addition of notes like vanilla, amber, and sandalwood, says perfumer Bérénice Watteau. "Many powdery perfumes have a velvety and enveloping feel on the skin, making them a great fit for anybody who is looking for a warm, creamy fragrance,” she explains. If this type of scent sounds appealing, then you're in the right place, because team Cosmo rounded up the best powdery perfumes. We've personally sniffed, spritzed, and fallen in love with each one, and can confidently say there's a perfume on the list for everyone.

Keep scrolling for the best powdery perfumes that will make you feel like a sophisticated angel. 👼

One Cosmo editor says: “Honey perfumes are difficult to get right. Many can smell too sticky or syrupy. Turns out, a swirl of powder—in the form of heliotrope, coconut, and iris—was all that was needed to make the note live up to its liquid gold reputation. I feel like I’ve jumped tax brackets when I spritz this scent. (I’m manifesting!) It’s delicately sweet and airy, and lasts forever. Seriously, all I need is one spritz, and I can still smell it on myself after 12 hours.”—MARY HONKUS, CONTRIBUTING BEAUTY EDITOR

One Cosmo editor says: "I always thought powdery perfumes would make me smell like a grandma, but either I'm inching toward grandma status or Angel Dust by Fugazzi is built different. It's powdery but not from heavy florals. Instead, the scent combines a mix of skin-like cashmere, musk, and amber. It's soft and reminiscent of the inside of a knitwear shop.”—BETH GILLETTE, BEAUTY EDITOR

One Cosmo editor says: “Have you ever wondered what the clouds smell like? Well, scientists confirmed the aroma is fluffy, powdery, soft, and sparkling. Future Society stuck a petri dish out of a plane to capture cloud microbes to create a photo-realistic perfume inspired by the clouds. (I love science!) This carries an airy aura as sweet sugar, sparkling aldehydes, and aquatic blooms tickle my nose. The dry down feels atmospheric and clean thanks to delicate musks.”—MH

One Cosmo editor says: “In my experience, powdery perfumes are some of the most polarizing. (People either love or hate them.) Everyone seems to adore Guerlain’s take on powder when I wear L’Heure Bleue, though. Every time I mist this ethereal perfume, the compliments roll in. The ozonic-oceanic notes ripple like waves as they mingle with hints of powdery iris and the most delicate vanilla. Thank goodness the price is relatively affordable compared to other scents from the luxury house because I’ve nearly finished my bottle in three months.”—MH

One Cosmo editor says: "Glossier You Fleur is hands-down my fave fragrance from the brand. If Gen Z was a powder perfume, it would be this. Yes, it's floral, but not in a matronly rose way. It boasts the same scent composition as the OG Glossier You, but it also has ylang-ylang and osmanthus to sweeten it. Plus, notes of ambrox and cashmeran ground it with a warm and modern powdery vibe."—JASMINE HYMAN, ASSISTANT BEAUTY EDITOR

One Cosmo editor says: “This is the most powdery perfume I own, to the point that it honestly smells like baby powder—if there was such a thing as expensive baby powder for rich lil nepo babies. It's not your standard rose-y, mature powder perfume. Instead, there's clove, plum, vanilla, and cashmere that give it a sensual musky aura."—BG

One Cosmo editor says: "Chanel is basically synonymous with powder perfumes, and Chance eau Tendre is Chanel’s most citrusy, fresh scent, per our testing. But don’t let the fruitiness fool you—this could so easily be worn year-round, for either day or night. The added musk and rose really balances it all out and leaves you smelling like you just took the best everything shower."—BG

One Cosmo editor says: "Prada Paradoxe is the epitome of a sexy and juicy perfume that has a surprising powdery finish to it. If you want to smell flirty, floral, and fruity without being overly sweet, I could not recommend this scent more. It opens with notes of tangerine, pear, and jasmine, then it dries down with amber and musk to leave behind a warm and skin-like powdery aroma. It's my favorite nighttime fragrance when I want to smell straight up irresistible."—JH

Watteau says that just like adding a touch of sea salt to chocolate to bring out its flavor, choosing a fragrance with contrasting notes will help powder perfumes really shine. “Powdery notes are fluffy, enveloping a super soft and comforting feeling if they’re well balanced. Without this element of contrast it can become dense and heavy or dry and suffocating,” she says. She also recommends going for something with a complementary green, citrus, or woody note to allow the fragrance to “breathe” and become more dimensional.

Powdery perfumes have evolved in recent years and because of this, the category has expanded in surprising ways. That means if you like florals, earthy scents, spicy fragrances, or even deep, more mysterious aromas, you'll be able to find a powdery perfume for your vibe. I recommend shopping by your your primary fragrance category first, and then look for words like "soft" "smooth" or "powdery" in the description.

#6875. Survey: Organizations Report Positive ROI on AI Investments, Yet Data Readiness and Oversight Lag Behind

According to a survey by data and AI services provider Further, enterprises are reporting benefits from artificial intelligence even as their data preparation and oversight processes remain incomplete.

The company's recent State of Enterprise AI Maturity research surveyed 515 U.S.-based AI decision-makers. Just 5% believed their organizations' data was largely ready for AI.

The findings suggest that gaining value from individual AI applications and preparing to operate them more broadly are separate challenges. Reported returns alone do not establish that an organization has the foundations to sustain or expand those results.

Further found that 37% of respondents' organizations had paused, scaled back, or abandoned an underperforming AI initiative in the previous 12 months. Among that group, data quality or availability was the most frequently cited main reason, at 24%.

Meanwhile, 29% reported completing a formal data audit and remediation plan. The research also placed 69% of organizations in the exploring, experimenting, or implementing stages of adoption.

Further distinguishes data prepared for conventional reporting from data prepared for AI. Its explanation emphasizes data origins and documentation, along with access controls that support ongoing evaluation and governance.

For readers assessing their own readiness, the practical issue is whether data supports the requirements of a particular application. A collection suitable for a dashboard may need additional preparation before it can reliably support an AI system.

Seventy-seven percent reported a positive return on AI investment. However, only 3% said their organizations had a documented AI-audit process.

The findings measure different things: perceived financial benefit and the existence of a repeatable oversight process. Neither substitutes for the other.

"In reality, confidence without a documented process is exposure," said Kierra Dotson, associate director of AI strategy and governance at Further. "Closing that gap doesn't take new technology; it requires an auditable governance process."

For the full report, visit the Further site (registration required).

#6870. Researchers Find AI Is Accelerating Vulnerability Discovery

AI-powered vulnerability research is contributing to a major increase in security updates, raising concerns that IT teams could fall behind as patching demands grow, according to cybersecurity company ThreatDown.

In a recent blog post, the company stated that in September 2026, vendors addressed 998 common vulnerabilities and exposures (CVEs) around Patch Tuesday, compared with 96 during the same period last year. In addition, the three-month rolling average of patched Linux kernel vulnerabilities tripled year over year, while Google's Chrome browser experienced a 36-fold increase.

The researchers point to advanced AI models, including Anthropic's Claude Mythos Preview, as a major factor behind the increase in vulnerability discovery.

Introduced in April 2026, Mythos Preview is being used through Project Glasswing, an initiative involving Microsoft, the Linux Foundation and other organizations to identify vulnerabilities in critical software.

While AI could ultimately make software more secure, the cybersecurity company warns that vulnerability discovery is accelerating faster than organizations can deploy fixes.

IT teams must still evaluate patches, test compatibility and schedule updates without disrupting critical business operations.

The challenge is particularly significant for organizations already struggling with patch management.

The cybersecurity vendor cites Verizon's 2026 Data Breach Investigations Report, which found that 35% of known actively exploited vulnerabilities remained unpatched after 28 days in 2025.

Ryan Powell, vice president of IT and security at ThreatDown, said automated patching systems can help organizations manage the growing workload. However, additional patches still require more testing and validation, increasing operational overhead and the risks associated with frequent system changes.

The report also highlights the risks of vulnerabilities in widely used software components such as OpenSSL, embedded in applications, network devices, and security products.

Unlike standalone applications, these components often depend on downstream vendors to release updates before organizations can deploy fixes.

The security firm's analysis points to CVE-2022-0778, an OpenSSL vulnerability patched in March 2022, as an example. Some affected products did not receive updates until several months later.

As AI accelerates vulnerability discovery, these dependencies could delay remediation and extend organizations' exposure to known security flaws.

ThreatDown's findings illustrate a growing challenge for enterprise cybersecurity: AI is making vulnerabilities easier to discover but fixing them remains a complex and time-consuming process.

For the full report, visit the ThreatDown blog here.

The federal Education Freedom Tax Credit (EFTC) is a new source of funding for education that could benefit 90% of students in states that have opted in to the program. However, some confusion and misconceptions exist surrounding details of the program, including which programs may qualify, how parents can apply for scholarships, and how public school students can benefit.

Class CrunchLabs, the suite of free curriculum resources from NASA engineer turned YouTube science communicator Mark Rober, has released its first two courses on YouTube.

AI agents have moved into mainstream enterprise use, but the content infrastructure needed to support them has struggled to keep up, according to a new report from cloud content management company Box.

#6863. Ransomware Attacks Move Beyond Enterprise Systems to Theaten Employees' Families

Attackers are exploiting enterprises' own artificial intelligence systems and extending pressure beyond internal networks to employees and their families, according to a new Semperis survey of organizations that suffered disruptive ransomware attacks.

In a recent announcement, the company highlighted findings from its 2026 Ransomware Risk Report, which examines AI-related attack tactics, personal intimidation, and recovery preparedness. Censuswide surveyed 1,200 information technology and security professionals across nine countries and eight industry sectors in the first half of 2026.

The sample has an important limit: This year's study included only organizations where at least one ransomware attack caused business disruption. The results describe those victims' experiences, rather than the likelihood that an organization will suffer an attack. The report says that sampling approach changed from previous studies.

Semperis sells identity security, recovery and crisis management products.

The report says 84% of respondents experienced attacks that exploited AI in some way. Its breakdown distinguishes AI-based attacks, reported by 40%, from attacks that manipulated victims' own AI agents or systems, reported by 44%. That distinction matters: The broader figure covers both attackers using AI and attackers exploiting AI deployed inside an organization.

Defensive use of AI also produced mixed responses. Nearly half of respondents, 49%, said they successfully used AI for detection or response, while 23% said their AI tools failed to detect or respond successfully. Those are survey responses about victimized organizations, rather than a controlled comparison of security products.

Chris Inglis, former U.S. national cyber director and a Semperis strategic adviser, described the difficulty of recognizing AI's involvement. "Many attacks are not perceived as being aided and abetted by AI because they're just too slick, too sophisticated," he said in the report.

Data erasure, destruction, or release remained the most frequently reported threat, at 56%. Blackmail, extortion or public shaming followed at 41%, with system lockouts or operational sabotage at 40%. The report also recorded psychological intimidation at 23% and physical threats at 17%.

Artificial intelligence is saving scientists significant amounts of time, but researchers are giving some of those gains back by checking the technology's work, according to a new study involving Google, Google DeepMind, and MIT.

Our nation's largest early childhood program has not fully caught up with what science now tells us about how children learn.

In an active social engineering campaign, cyber attackers are impersonating IT help desks and using fake passkey setup requests to compromise employee identities and gain access to enterprise cloud data.

Increasingly, cyber criminals are sharing AI prompts, jailbreak techniques, and access to existing models that can be incorporated into fraud and phishing operations, according the latest AI Threat Report from Flashpoint.

#6859. Why You’re Still Getting Acne in Your 20s, 30s, and 40s, According to Dermatologists

Acne experts break down adult acne and how to treat it.

Throughout my teenage years, I was blessedly acne-free. I experienced severe eczema, and for that, I thought the trade-off for my dryness and rashes was the rarity of a pimple. Then I turned 22 and my face became a hotbed for breakouts, so much so that I started taking oral spironolactone and underwent an entire skincare program with Face Reality to get everything under control.

Now I’m 26, and while I am off the daily meds and have the ability to be more liberal with my regimen, I still experience breakouts across my chin from time to time. When I spoke with friends and found out so many of them were also experiencing acne for the first time and going on Accutane and spironolactone, I concluded that adult acne is not nearly as addressed as teenage acne.

Of course, for teenagers, breakouts can be difficult and stressful, but they’re common, and skincare brands like Starface and Hero Cosmetics have helped normalize pimple patches and mitigate the insecurity of having acne. But for adults in their 20s, 30s, and 40s who also experience breakouts, acne is far less discussed.

“I think one of the biggest misconceptions about acne is that it's mostly a teenage skin condition,” says Stephanie Criscione, licensed aesthetician and senior director of education at Face Reality. “Acne can show up at almost any stage of life, and someone who’s 35 may need a very different approach than someone who’s 15, even if their breakouts look similar.”

Below, I chatted with board-certified dermatologist Karan Lal, MD, and licensed aestheticians Samantha Susca and Criscione for all their insight on the different phases of acne and what treatment might look like throughout every age.

“Teenage acne is often driven by the hormonal surge associated with puberty, which increases oil production and contributes to clogged follicles and inflammation,” Criscione explains. “We tend to see a combination of blackheads, whiteheads, and inflammatory lesions, often affecting larger areas of the face and sometimes the chest and back.”

Susca adds, “the increase of certain androgen production and sebaceous activity, coupled with the lifestyle of a teenager, which is often not the most hygienic or knowledgeable about personal care,” can contribute to breakouts.

Dr. Lal adds that teenage acne “tends to involve a broader distribution across the face, particularly the T-zone, and can also affect the chest and back.”

Unfortunately, leaving your teenage years doesn’t always mean leaving acne behind. Our experts all agree that adult acne can be more complicated to treat because there isn’t a one-size-fits-all solution, and the root causes can vary. “Acne that arises in your 20s and 30s is more likely to be due to hormonal fluctuations, which are not necessarily always hormonal imbalances,” Susca explains.

“Fluctuations can occur from things like age and stress, pregnancies, stopping or starting contraceptives or other medications, as well as genetic and family history of perimenopause, which can happen as early as in the 30s age range,” Susca says. “Inflammation paired with stress, lifestyle, travel, and using an incorrect routine can be factors that contribute as well.”

In terms of what these breakouts actually look like, “We tend to see more persistent inflammatory acne in some adults, particularly around the lower face,” says Criscione. This includes the jawline and the chin, which are areas most commonly associated with hormonal acne.

Dr. Lal adds: “These breakouts can be persistent, cyclical, and more likely to leave behind post-inflammatory hyperpigmentation or scarring.”

And something that often gets overlooked is that adult skin has competing needs, which can further compromise the skin barrier and contribute to persistent acne. “Someone in their 30s may be dealing with breakouts while simultaneously experiencing dehydration, sensitivity, pigmentation, or early signs of aging,” Criscione says. “Unfortunately, reaching for the strongest acne treatment on the shelf can end up compromising the skin barrier and making those other concerns worse.” So it’s important to be selective about the products addressing other skin concerns if you’re also dealing with acne at this stage.

“Unfortunately, turning 40 or 50 doesn’t automatically mean we’re in the clear, pun intended,” Criscione says. Hormonal changes can continue to influence the skin well into midlife, and acne can take on a different appearance during this stage. “Perimenopausal and menopausal acne is often very red and inflamed, presenting as cystic in many cases,” Susca explains.

“As estrogen levels fluctuate and eventually decline, the relative influence of androgens on the sebaceous glands may become more pronounced,” says Dr. Lal. “Some patients experience new or worsening acne despite having had relatively clear skin for years.”

“In menopause, the skin also often becomes drier, thinner, and more sensitive,” explains Dr. Lal. “This creates an interesting clinical challenge, where patients may simultaneously experience acne and reduced skin barrier function, so treatment must address breakouts without unnecessarily compromising hydration or skin integrity.”

According to Dr. Lal, acne treatment should be individualized based on several factors, including age, acne severity, skin type, hormonal status, and risk of scarring.

For teenagers, treatment often starts by addressing excess oil and clogged pores. “In teenagers, I often focus on establishing a simple, consistent routine,” explains Dr. Lal. Classic acne-fighting ingredients like “benzoyl peroxide can be particularly helpful for inflammatory acne, and salicylic acid may be useful for patients with predominantly clogged pores. More severe cases may require prescription therapies,” he adds.

“In the 20s and 30s age range, treatment often needs to address both acne and the patient’s concerns about pigmentation, texture, and early photoaging,” says Dr. Lal. “Topical retinoids are valuable, while azelaic acid can be an excellent option for patients who experience both acne and post-inflammatory hyperpigmentation.” In some cases for women, he adds that hormonal prescriptions such as spironolactone or certain combined oral contraceptives may be beneficial.

“For patients in their 40s and beyond, I place greater emphasis on balancing efficacy with tolerability,” says Dr. Lal. “The skin may be drier or more sensitive, so I may recommend a lower-strength retinoid, less frequent application, or a more supportive moisturizing regimen.”

Dr. Lal also emphasizes that treatment should account for the specific type of acne someone is experiencing. “For comedonal acne, retinoids are particularly important; for inflammatory acne, benzoyl peroxide and prescription anti-inflammatory treatments may be needed, and for deep nodular or scarring acne, systemic treatment—including isotretinoin in appropriate patients—may be necessary.”

“Androgens, which are present in both men and women, stimulate oil production in the skin,” says Dr. Lal. “During puberty, that increase in oil can contribute to clogged follicles and inflammatory breakouts, and as we move into adulthood, fluctuations associated with menstrual cycles, pregnancy, and perimenopause can influence oil production and inflammation, sometimes triggering acne in people who haven't experienced breakouts in years.”

“Acne is not simply a teenage problem,” Dr. Lal assures. “It is a condition that can change in its presentation and treatment needs throughout adulthood.”

There are several different types of acne, ranging from clogged pores to deeper, more painful inflammatory breakouts. Here’s how to tell them apart.

Comedonal acne: “Comedonal acne is generally what we think of as blackheads and whiteheads,” explains Criscione. “These are caused by excess oil and dead skin cells.” Blackheads are open comedones, while whiteheads are closed comedones. Susca adds that comedonal acne typically “looks like skin-colored bumps under the skin and it’s often associated with hormonal acne in teenagers and in early 20s.”

Inflammatory acne: “This includes papules and pustules, which can appear as red, raised bumps or lesions containing visible pus,” says Dr. Lal. “inflammatory breakouts can sometimes leave behind pigmentation or scarring.” Criscione adds that “More severe inflammatory acne can involve deeper nodules or cysts that are painful, persistent, appear like hardening lumps under the skin.”

Nodular or cystic acne: Susca describes this category of acne as “deep, hardened, painful inflammatory lesions beneath the skin, often with a higher risk of scarring or pigmentation.” Dr. Lal says “the term ‘cystic acne’ is commonly used by patients, although many deep lesions are technically inflammatory nodules rather than true cysts.”

Hormonal acne: Susca explains that “hormonal acne isn’t actually a separate lesion type, it's a description of acne influenced by hormonal activity, and it can present as anything from clogged pores to deeper inflammatory breakouts.”

“Hormonal acne is often associated with breakouts along the chin, jawline, and lower cheeks, particularly when breakouts are deep, tender, and tend to appear around the same time each month,” Criscione says.

If you’re experiencing adult acne at any stage of life, know that you’re not alone. Millions of people experience breakouts well beyond their teenage years, and acne can look different as your skin and hormones change over time.

Consider speaking with a skincare professional who can help identify what’s contributing to your breakouts and recommend the right ingredients, products, and treatments for your skin. Adult acne is common, treatable, and, most importantly, nothing to be embarrassed about!

Jasmine Hyman is the assistant beauty editor at Cosmo who covers all things skincare and has dealt with acne for the last five years. For this story, she consulted acne experts for all their insight on addressing and treating acne at all stages of life.

You’ll also likely find her in bed reading a good book or endlessly scrolling TikTok (spoiler: it’s usually the latter) while listening to Harry Styles’ entire discography on repeat. Follow her on Insta to be inundated with pictures of her meals.

#6856. IDC Report Finds Significant Gap Between AI Spending and Measurable Financial Returns

Nearly 70% of organizations report a significant gap between artificial intelligence spending and measurable financial returns, according to a recent survey by IDC.

The research firm's 2026 Worldwide AI Economics and Cost of Ownership Survey found that 69% reported such a gap, while only 15% had achieved an AI return on investment exceeding 100%.

In a separate May 2026 survey, 73% of respondents identified excessive AI costs as a significant risk.

IDC highlighted the findings in an October 20 analysis examining what enterprise customers expect from AI technology vendors.

Enterprise customers are placing greater emphasis on measurable business outcomes when evaluating AI products, according to IDC.

The firm identified three priorities for vendors: demonstrating financial value, addressing governance and security requirements, and building support beyond IT departments.

The analysis also highlighted the gap between initial agentic AI deployments and broader adoption. Although 80% of organizations have started deploying agentic AI, only 17% are using it across the enterprise.

IDC said evaluating AI investments can be difficult when the technology changes how employees work and how business processes operate.

The firm expects AI infrastructure capacity to catch up with demand around 2029, potentially easing costs. Until then, vendors face pressure to demonstrate the long-term financial value of their products.

AI funding decisions involve a broad range of executives. IDC's September 2026 C-Suite Tech Survey found that CIOs and CTOs were involved in 63% of those decisions, business-function executives in 62%, and chief financial officers in 61%.

A separate IDC survey found that 42% of CEOs planned to hire a chief AI officer within the next 12 months.

Buyers also reported concerns about the products and terms vendors offer. Limited integration with existing systems, insufficient model and data transparency, and concerns about vendor lock-in were each cited by 34% of respondents.

Overpromising and underdelivering, along with unclear pricing models, were each cited by 31%.

IDC said vendors need to substantiate financial returns and address security and governance requirements while working with decision-makers across IT, finance, and other business departments.

For more information, go to the IDC blog.

Artificial intelligence is saving scientists significant amounts of time, but researchers are giving some of those gains back by checking the technology's work, according to a new study involving Google, Google DeepMind, and MIT.

Our nation's largest early childhood program has not fully caught up with what science now tells us about how children learn.

In an active social engineering campaign, cyber attackers are impersonating IT help desks and using fake passkey setup requests to compromise employee identities and gain access to enterprise cloud data.

Increasingly, cyber criminals are sharing AI prompts, jailbreak techniques, and access to existing models that can be incorporated into fraud and phishing operations, according the latest AI Threat Report from Flashpoint.

#6854. Benzoyl Peroxide Is the Unsung Hero of Acne Treatments, So Why Is It So Controversial?

Derms and aestheticians agree...but very few brands have caught on.

We may earn commission from links on this page, but we only recommend products we love. Promise.

In the early aughts, the options for acne treatments were limited but surprisingly effective. When I got my first breakout, my mom dragged me to CVS, where I stocked up on salicylic acid washes, witch hazel toners, apricot scrubs (IYKYK), and benzoyl peroxide spot treatments. When all of that didn’t work, we moved on to Proactiv, the three-step infomercial darling allegedly loved by the likes of Kelly Clarkson and Jessica Simpson. It also featured a benzoyl peroxide–spiked wash and spot treatment plus an exfoliating toner. Back then, curing your acne was fairly simple. Kill bacteria with benzoyl peroxide, then exfoliate with salicylic acid. But as skincare has advanced, and consumers have wised up to what ingredients actually do, we left benzoyl peroxide by the wayside. Why?

Earlier this year, still dealing with acne as I approached 30, I got a facial with aesthetician and self-proclaimed acne whisperer Sofie Pavitt. Her recommendation was simple: Wash four nights a week with a benzoyl peroxide cleanser (any drugstore option was fine, she told me), and pat on a mandelic acid serum every morning (she likes her own brand, of course). It sounded too good to be true. I’ve had hormonal acne since I was 11—something so simple couldn’t be the fix. But 10 months later, my skin has never been clearer, brighter, or more even.

I almost entirely credit my lack of breakouts to benzoyl peroxide, which if you didn’t know, helps kill acne-causing bacteria on the surface of your skin. I’ll shout it from the rooftops and to anyone who will listen that benzoyl peroxide cured my acne. But as I scroll through my inbox at every new acne launch, I’m confused why so few brands ever launch any products with the magic ingredient. I can count on one hand, literally, how many products have come out this year highlighting benzoyl peroxide. The list is vast for the other famed acne treatments: salicylic acid, tea tree oil, azelaic acid, niacinamide, sulfur, glycolic acid, and lactic acid.

But “dermatologists have never, never left,” dermatologist Heather Woolery-Lloyd, MD, tells me. “We always loved benzoyl peroxide because it works so well and is predictable,” she says. “It’s a hero when it comes to acne.” So where the heck did it go? And why is no one talking about it?

To understand benzoyl peroxide, you have to first understand how acne works. When you have acne-prone skin, your pores get very sticky with oil. You get a buildup of oil and bacteria in that pore, which is what turns into pimples. Benzoyl peroxide kills the acne-causing bacteria in that pore by releasing oxygen into the pore, breaking apart the bacteria, explains Dr. Woolery-Lloyd. It’s also a comedolytic, which, Dr. Woolery-Lloyd explains, means it can gently break down dead skin cells to reduce clogged pores.

First of all, it’s pretty difficult to use in formulating, Dr. Woolery-Lloyd explains. “It’s not the easiest thing to just throw into any lotion and know that it will be cosmetically elegant to apply to skin,” she says. “Dermatologists might think of it as a hero, but from a cosmetic scientist’s standpoint, it is a little more complicated to work with. It’s easier to use salicylic acid.”

But there’s also some red tape. Both benzoyl peroxide and salicylic acid are considered “over-the-counter drugs,” hence why there are drug facts on the back of the packaging. This just means that brands have to prove that they’re stable through independent testing. It’s good for the consumer, Dr. Woolery-Lloyd says, because then you know the product has the right percentage and that percentage is stable until its expiration date. But ingredients like niacinamide and azelaic acid can be added without the same regulatory burden. “It’s much easier to throw niacinamide into a product than add benzoyl peroxide,” says Dr. Woolery-Lloyd.

Maybe it’s my bias here, but I sense a benzoyl peroxide–aissance will be upon us shortly. Sofie Pavitt’s eponymous skincare line features the only benzoyl peroxide product at Sephora: a 5 percent spot treatment called Reset. She reformulated it from an allover face mask and relaunched this year, and it’s been one of my go-to spot treatments in 2026. But until the chic Sephora brands catch up, the drugstore is holding it down on the BP front. There’s PanOxyl, which is known for its 4 percent and 10 percent benzoyl peroxide cleansers (that’s what I use, FYI), as well as a new 10 percent spot treatment. CeraVe also offers a creamy 4 percent benzoyl peroxide cleanser with niacinamide and hyaluronic acid. An oldie but a goodie is Clean & Clear’s Persa-Gel 10, a 10 percent BP spot treatment gel that layers well under makeup. If that’s a bit too harsh for your skin, try La Roche-Posay’s 5.5 percent BP spot treatment.

Sofie Pavitt Face 5% Reset Benzoyl Peroxide Acne Treatment

PanOxyl Acne Creamy Wash, 4% Benzoyl Peroxide

Clean & Clear Persa-Gel 10 Acne Spot Treatment

La Roche-Posay Effaclar BPO Multi-Target Acne Treatment

Recently, prescription acne-treatment Epiduo, which combines 0.1 percent adapalene (a retinoid for acne) with 2.5 percent benzoyl peroxide, became available over the counter, which is guaranteed to be a game changer. “It’s really exciting for us because it is the most effective over-the-counter acne treatment now,” says Dr. Woolery-Lloyd. “A few months ago, I’d have to write a prescription, and now you can walk into your local Walmart, Target, CVS, or Walgreens or even go on Amazon and buy it over the counter,” she says.

The formula rose to popularity because it has a unique delivery base, which “evenly suspends both of the active ingredients,” says Dr. Woolery-Lloyd. “This prevents hot spots where you get local irritation.” Both retinoids and benzoyl peroxide can be drying, which is why they typically aren’t advised to be used together (and you shouldn’t try to cocktail it at home), but this gel base helps. You also only have to use it once per day, which Dr. Woolery-Lloyd loves since she knows that consistency is the key to all acne treatments.

Neither is necessarily better than the other, because they do different things. Benzoyl peroxide kills acne-causing bacteria on the surface of your skin, reducing the likelihood that bacteria can clog and inflame your pores. Salicylic acid, on the other hand, is an oil-soluble exfoliant. It goes into your pores and helps clear out the dead skin and oils that clog them. Both can be beneficial in an acne regimen, to be fair.

But it’s hard not to play favorites, for both me and Dr. Woolery-Lloyd. “When it comes to acne, benzoyl peroxide is my preferred ingredient for sure,” she says. And I agree. It’s the winner for my oily skin and persistent hormonal acne.

As much as I love benzoyl peroxide, I understand it can be drying, especially for those with already dry skin. To cut down on irritation and dryness, Dr. Woolery-Lloyd recommends opting for a lower concentration. “Benzoyl peroxide comes in many different concentrations over the counter, but the studies show that you don't really have to use those higher concentrations to get the efficacy,” she explains. The sweet spot is between 2.5 and 5 percent; you really don’t need to go with a 10 percent formula to get the benefits.

As far as the rest of your skincare routine with benzoyl peroxide, Dr. Woolery-Lloyd suggests a pH-balanced cleanser (on the days you don’t use a benzoyl peroxide formula) and moisturizer to help keep your skin barrier healthy. You can actually use moisturizer before or after your benzoyl peroxide formula (especially if you’re using something like Epiduo, which also contains a retinoid). “I personally always put medicine first and the moisturizer on top, but it actually doesn't matter as long as you're using a moisturizer that can help to minimize that risk of irritation,” says Dr. Woolery-Lloyd.

To improve tolerability, you can also start with every other day, or every third day, then increase if your skin reacts well. You can also try using a wash only once or twice a week. That’s what I do, and I opt for a hydrating cleanser on the off nights with lots of hydration and barrier care to offset any irritation, and my skin has handled it well. In fact, my skin looks the best it ever has right now. Thank you, benzoyl peroxide!

Heather Woolery-Lloyd, MD, is a board-certified dermatologist based in Miami. She is currently the director of the Skin of Color Division for the University of Miami Department of Dermatology.

#6851. We’ve Tested Hundreds of Makeup Brands—These Help Cover Zits Without Causing Them

We may earn commission from links on this page, but we only recommend products we love. Promise.

You can use all of the best acne products in the world, but if you’re dabbing on makeup that clogs your pores every day, your dreams of clear skin might not be a reality. Makeup makes a huge difference for acne, far beyond how well it covers up your breakouts. Some formulas are filled with emollients that make the product creamy and dewy, which is great for dry skin, but can potentially clog your pores if you’re prone to breakouts.

A few years ago, I ran every single one of my makeup products through the pore-clogger checker from Acne Clinic NYC. And to my dismay, many of my favorites just weren’t right for my skin. Since then, I’ve vowed to only ever put non-acnegenic products on my face, which has come with a lot of trial and error to find the best makeup brands for acne.

A lot of brands have one or two products that work well for my skin, but only a few really take the cake for their entire lines. If one of the below brands launches a complexion product, I can (usually) safely assume that it won’t break me out. Many of the below brands also make high-performing formulas for covering up your acne without exacerbating texture or redness. From lightweight foundations to smoothing blushes to high-coverage concealers, the below brands have makeup for acne down pat.

Cofounded by makeup artist Nikki DeRoest, Ciele was founded with two missions: acne-friendly formulas that put sun protection at the forefront. While everything is rid of pore-clogging ingredients, they do contain SPF 50+. None of these are a replacement for your daily sunscreen, but added protection doesn’t hurt. In fact, sun exposure can irritate skin, potentially trigger breakouts, and even prolong or worsen hyperpigmentation. The brand has a full assortment of skincare-infused products, including the serum foundation, a Cosmo editor favorite.

Ciele Tint & PROTECT SPF 50+ Tinted Serum Foundation

Ciele Blush & PROTECT SPF 50+ Liquid Blush

Ciele Bronze & PROTECT SPF 50+ Bronzing Concentrate

Many with acne and eczema-prone skin have sworn off makeup entirely, assuming that even formula’s suited to their skin type will feel too heavy or make flaking and texture look worse. That is precisely why Amy Liu founded Tower 28, a makeup line infused with skincare that won’t make your skin look worse when you wear it. They’re the first brand to get the National Eczema Association’s seal on a makeup product ever with their Swipe Serum Non-Acnegenic Foundation (which Cosmo editors gave rave reviews).

Since their launch in 1995, the ethos of Bare Minerals has always been natural and skin-forward. The brand’s original tagline was “makeup so pure, you could sleep in it.” Thirty years later, the brand is still one of our favorites for covering zits. Obviously, the iconic powder foundation is a go-to. The formula builds up for coverage and sets with a skin-like finish. But they’ve also launched quite a few liquid and cream products that are equally as lightweight with a mix of light to medium coverage.

bareMinerals Original Loose Powder Foundation SPF 15

Although Benefit became known in later years for their mascaras and Benetint lip stain, the makeup and skincare brand is pretty invested in pores. In 2010, Benefit launched the POREfessional Primer, a velvety, silicone-based smoothing makeup primer to help reduce the look of texture and pores. They’ve expanded into skincare with the same pore-treating philosophy, but their newest makeup launches have cemented the brand as acne-friendly. The POREfessional Foundation, a Cosmo Acne Awards winner in 2025, and POREfessional Concealer both offer full-coverage without aggravating acne.

Beyond offering formulas that are suitable for acne, Urban Decay’s complexion assortment keeps oily skin in mind. You might know the brand from your Naked Palette days (never forget), but they’ve really cornered the market on long-wearing, mattifying base products. There’s a mattifying setting spray, primer, and even an innovative powder spray (!).

Both the cream blush and bronzer are not only made with acne-friendly ingredients, but they also blend out evenly and make textured skin look smooth. We also love the brand’s mist, which is technically a skincare product, but it melts down your makeup for a dewy finish that won’t clog pores.

#6850. Report: Global Ransomware Attacks Rise 23% in Q3

Global ransomware activity increased 23% in the third quarter of 2026, with organizations in North America and Europe accounting for the largest share of attacks, according to cybersecurity firm NCC Group.

The company's Cyber Threat Intelligence Report Review of Q3 2026 recorded 2,865 ransomware victim claims, up from 2,325 in the previous quarter.

Despite the quarterly increase, attacks declined 26% month over month in September to 821. North America recorded the most attacks that month with 390, followed by Europe with 211.

The industrial sector accounted for 29% of quarterly attacks, while Qilin remained the most active ransomware group for the sixth consecutive quarter, recording 365 victims. The Gentlemen followed with 356, and INC Ransom ranked third with 109.

The report highlighted a July cyber attack against a small UK power generation facility that forced operations to stop for approximately four days.

Although the incident did not affect the national electricity grid, it demonstrated how cyber attacks can disrupt physical infrastructure.

The security researchers said the incident was consistent with techniques previously associated with Iranian-affiliated campaigns, including attacks against internet-exposed industrial controllers.

However, the UK government has not formally attributed the attack, and the techniques used remain unconfirmed.

The report also identified growing security challenges as organizations connect traditional IT networks with operational technology (OT) systems for remote maintenance and centralized management.

The firm recommended network segmentation, stronger privileged-access controls, and continuous monitoring of OT environments.

The research found that more than 40 vulnerabilities affecting enterprise applications, VPN appliances, e-mail gateways, and management platforms were added to CISA's Known Exploited Vulnerabilities Catalog during the quarter.

The report examined Gunra, a ransomware-as-a-service operation that has targeted 53 victims across multiple industries as of Q3.

According to the cybersecurity company, Gunra exploited vulnerabilities CVE-2024-55591 and CVE-2025-24472 in internet-facing Fortinet devices to gain access to corporate networks.

The group also used stolen credentials, session hijacking, and administrative tools to move between systems before deploying ransomware.

Other emerging ransomware groups included Deadlock and Storm, with the latter claiming 78 victims by the end of September.

Researchers also observed initial access brokers advertising compromised VPN, Remote Desktop Protocol, and SSH access, alongside access to cloud identities, source code repositories, and security management platforms.

The quarterly review also highlighted BraZetsu, an AI-enhanced Windows malware framework that evolved into a specialized initial access toolkit.

According to the report, BraZetsu uses AI models to automate victim profiling, analyze compromised environments, and identify valuable corporate systems.

The framework searches for enterprise software, financial data, digital certificates, and cloud credentials, helping operators identify compromised access that can be sold to other cyber criminals.

The cybersecurity firm also identified risks involving legitimate AI agents, including prompt injection, excessive permissions, and unintended access to external systems.

The report cited incidents involving U.S. and Australian government systems as examples of agents accessing resources beyond their intended scope.

Researchers warned that these incidents do not necessarily involve malicious intent but can create consequences resembling conventional cyber intrusions.

For the full report, visit the NCC Group site.

#6845. The Women of ‘RHOSLC’ Should Let Bronwyn Newport Have a Little PR Relationship, as a Treat

In an era of reality television where the fourth wall has essentially become a pile of rubble, Bravolebrities have a new favorite game: calling each other out for self-producing storylines. And Bronwyn Newport of The Real Housewives of Salt Lake City is the latest to find herself tied to the stake in this authenticity witch hunt.

Her fellow Utahns seem to believe her relationship with 33-year-old model Brandon Good—which began two months after she announced the end of her nine-year marriage to Todd Bradley and fizzled out before the season aired—is a publicity stunt.

As expected, Reddit threads are absolutely flooded with receipts, proof, timelines, and screenshots supporting both sides of the argument. But there’s one response that I’ve been surprised to see missing from the conversation, and that’s a GIF of Arrested Development’s Lucille Bluth enthusiastically boasting “Good for her!”

If you’re not fluent in reaction memes, let me explain.

It doesn’t really matter if this was a bona fide relationship rebound or a carefully-calculated chess move. In either case, this alleged ploy is exactly what we all need from this show. Brandon is a fun addition to the mix. His mere presence ruffles the cast’s feathers in a very low-stakes manner, which is a welcomed reprieve in a season that’s getting darker by the week.

It’s silly to see Bronwyn sweat bullets through feathered couture while her unfiltered mother, Muzzy, grills Brandon through every second of their probably-too-soon first encounter. And when Meredith Marks and Lisa Barlow crooned through their first live performance of Hypocrisy and Bullshit, it was objectively hilarious that sweet little Zoolander found the song to be catchy before realizing it was an attack on his very own girlfriend.

If she had decided to play coy and keep her romantic life out of the spotlight, she’d have been accused of hiding something. But because Bronwyn opted to bring a new fling into the mix, she’s faced with having to beat the PR relationship allegations. And we all know that if she went for a secret, more sinister third thing—like getting really into aerial yoga or launching a luxury candle company—her cast members would have given her the same skepticism.

At the risk of getting too inside baseball, couldn’t one argue that a Housewife accusing her fellow cast member of faking a relationship for a storyline might also just be attempting to pull a narrative out of thin air? And furthermore, isn’t that what we’re all expecting these women to do when that sprinter van drops them off at a vacation rental in Palm Springs?

For 20 years, The Real Housewives have been giving us storylines that are barely tethered to reality. We’ve heard talk of clothing lines that never came to fruition, witnessed vow renewals that were just a divorce detour, and watched waiters hover around dinner tables where the goal is to unpack drama rather than to eat.

Whether Bronwnyn was acting in good faith or scheming, she read the brief, rolled up her designer sleeves, and came to work to get the job done. If you’ve ever fired off a “no worries!” Slack when you were, in fact, worrying, I think you can give her a pass on this one.

And let’s not forget that Bronwnyn spent last season dodging rumors that her 60-year-old estranged husband had farted while “making out” with another woman. If rolling up to the function with a boy toy who may or may not be sending an invoice her way afterwards is what it takes to recover from such a stinky dissolution of a marriage, so be it.

#6844. Microsoft Expands Protections and Controls for Sovereign Cloud

Microsoft is introducing new tools within its sovereign cloud offerings designed to give organizations tighter control over where their data resides, who can access it, and how critical systems operate, especially in light of AI becoming a bigger part of enterprise infrastructure.

In a recent announcement, Douglas Phillips, president and chief technology officer of Microsoft Specialized Clouds, outlined updates spanning public cloud, private cloud, and partner environments. The changes target governments, regulated industries, and organizations that need tighter control over sensitive workloads without abandoning cloud-based AI capabilities.

"Digital sovereignty should give organizations greater control and security over the technology they depend on, while continuing to innovate," Phillips wrote.

One of the biggest additions is Microsoft's Sovereign Control Panel, which will enter public preview at Microsoft Ignite. Available through the Azure portal, the tool will give IT administrators one place to manage data sovereignty settings, spot compliance issues, and identify potential risks that could disrupt operations.

Microsoft is also tightening controls over who can access sensitive systems. Its Data Guardian feature will require oversight by personnel based in Europe when Microsoft engineers access certain production systems in the European Union and European Free Trade Association. Another tool, Production Touch Ledger, will keep tamper-resistant records of that access, giving organizations a clearer picture of who accessed their systems and what they did.

Microsoft is also giving customers more control over encryption. External key management with Azure Managed HSM is now generally available, letting organizations keep their main encryption keys on hardware they own or manage outside Microsoft's cloud.

The company is also expanding its Sovereign Private Cloud offerings, giving organizations more options to run systems with or without an internet connection. New capabilities include GitHub Enterprise Local, GitHub Copilot with locally hosted AI models, and additional data services running on Azure Local.

The push for greater operational control comes as Microsoft is also reconsidering how enterprises should secure increasingly autonomous AI systems.

As reported by Redmond magazine, Microsoft is expanding Project Perception to address risks posed by what the company calls "agentic insiders," AI agents capable of accessing sensitive information and performing actions without constant human supervision.

That initiative introduces plans for Guardian Agents, specialized security agents designed to monitor other AI systems, evaluate their behavior and intervene when their actions become risky.

"Monitoring and securing those agents must become continuous," said Hayete Gallot, executive vice president of Microsoft Security.

Project Perception is about limiting what AI agents can do. Microsoft's sovereign cloud expansion tackles a different set of questions: where those systems run, who gets to administer them, and how the data stays protected.

Taken together, the two efforts point to a shift in Microsoft's enterprise AI strategy. The company is putting more weight on oversight, not just automation.

Phillips said AI is making these questions more pressing as organizations roll out intelligent systems across sensitive business processes and critical infrastructure.

For more information, go to the Microsoft blog.

Chris Paoli (@ChrisPaoli5) is the associate editor for Converge360.

#6837. Cisco Intros Privileged Infrastructure Access for People and AI Agents

Cisco has introduced Privileged Infrastructure Access, an approach to infrastructure administration that gives people and AI agents temporary, task-specific permissions instead of leaving standing privileges available after their work is done.

In a recent blog post, Cisco's Matt Caulfield described a system that uses cryptographic identity to connect an administrator or agent to the infrastructure needed for a particular task. Access is governed by short-lived privileges, with session recording providing a record of the work performed.

"If there is no work being done, no privilege exists," Caulfield wrote. Cisco positions the offering as a way to bring identity controls directly into infrastructure operations while reducing dependence on static credentials, shared secrets, and broad access.

The announcement follows Cisco's previously announced Teleport partnership and its Infrastructure Identity concept, which connects the identity of a person or machine with the resources it can access, the actions it performs, and the permissions governing those actions.

Caulfield described a gap between the controls organizations apply to workforce applications and those used to administer network infrastructure. Companies have invested in single sign-on, multifactor authentication, and risk-based controls for application access, he wrote, but those protections often do not follow an engineer logging into a router or switch.

Instead, infrastructure access may depend on long-lived credentials, shared accounts, or separate policy systems. According to Cisco, that separation makes it harder to enforce least privilege and establish which person or system performed a particular action.

The company illustrates the problem with an administrator updating hundreds of switches during a maintenance window. If some updates fail, the administrator needs to troubleshoot before the window closes. The permissions available to handle that work, however, may cover more infrastructure and remain active longer than the task requires.

Traditional privileged access management can reduce risk through credential vaulting and rotation, Caulfield wrote. But he argued that operational friction can undermine those controls when teams create exceptions to complete urgent work. Those exceptions can leave credentials and privileges usable after the immediate need has passed.

Cisco's stated objective is to give infrastructure teams a consistent route to the devices they manage while providing security teams with policy enforcement, temporary permissions, and a record of activity. The company describes operational convenience as part of the design, rather than treating access governance solely as a credential-management problem.

Privileged Infrastructure Access makes access an on-demand action, according to the announcement. Cryptographic identity establishes the actor, and the resulting connection provides only the privileges needed to complete the task. Those privileges expire when the task concludes.

For customers using Cisco Identity Services Engine (ISE) and Terminal Access Controller Access-Control System Plus (TACACS+), the offering can draw on existing policy and device context to govern access to Cisco routers and switches. Cisco says customers can extend the same access model to other infrastructure, including servers, Kubernetes clusters, and cloud resources.

#6833. Before & After: How an Artist and Architect Couple Turned the “Worst House” on the Block Into Their Dream Home

In Brooklyn, artist Katherine Keltner and architect Douglas Gauthier transform a crumbling townhouse with equal parts drama and restraint.

Before they stepped foot inside the crumbling brownstone on a landmark block in Brooklyn’s Boerum Hill, artist Katherine Keltner and architect Douglas Gauthier had toured 30 homes and browsed more than 200 online. Neither of them expected to become instantly captivated by the 1869 Italianate townhouse, which was being sold in a stately yet dilapidated condition, having once served as a single-room occupancy (SRO) residence. But it happened nonetheless.

“This was the first one that had the bones that Katherine wanted,” recalls Gauthier, who runs the firm GAuthier Architects. “It follows the classic real estate rule of buying the worst house on the best block.”

The strategy paid off: Once their offer was accepted, the couple embarked on an 18-month-long odyssey that saw them jump through a series of hoops (preservationists; concerned neighbors; skeptical engineers) to completely transform the moldering pile into a creative haven for them to live, work, and raise their daughter.

A true joint-effort, Gauthier oversaw a modest two-story rear expansion and structural alterations, while Keltner took charge of spatial arrangements as well as color, pattern, texture, and artwork—much of it her own.

Once deteriorating, the interiors now have an easy elegance, with plaster walls, arched passageways, and curved corners that give the soaring ceilings an intimate feel. Pink takes an unexpected starring role, appearing throughout the parlor floor and also in the zellige-clad primary bath, but other adventurous hues like mint green and crimson also feature prominently. Capping it off is a plywood-sheathed office for Gauthier’s practice, allowing the family retreat to double as a place of creative inspiration.

Below, Keltner and Gauthier share their biggest lessons (and some “before” pictures) from the painstaking passion project.

When the townhouse was converted to an SRO, it had been chopped up into at least 13 units and two kitchens, and few of the original decorative elements were still intact. A couple had bought it in the 1960s and made it a family home again and added an art gallery space. But by 2021, when Keltner and Gauthier first saw it, the house was in a state of severe neglect, with peeling paint, crumbling floors, ancient appliances—not to mention a 1,500-pound printing press in the studio (that the couple later preserved). Yet, Keltner and Gauthier saw an opportunity, not to recreate the past, but rather forge their own set of rules.

“The house wasn’t burdened with pristine historic details—our moldings were trashed,” says Gauthier. “There were a few things that we kept and celebrated, but to me, it was like a tabula rasa. We can just go in and make it ours.” Katherine agreed: “I feel like sometimes having to preserve things is more expensive than just starting clean,” she says. “And so I thought that was an advantage for us.”

Not having to be too precious with ornamentation allowed the couple to make a bold statement about crown moldings, eschewing tradition and installing curving corners and coved ceilings instead. Nowhere does the effect have more impact than in the parlor, where Gauthier devised a ceiling light in the shape of a giant oval that casts light upward rather than down. “I really like indirect lighting,” he says.

Crafted out of laser-cut plywood, the elliptical fixture is an abstracted homage to the ceiling medallions found in neighboring historic brownstones. “Some of them are incredibly ornate and beautiful, but they don't really have any use—they’re just decoration,” says Gauthier. “Adding ambient lighting to the living space takes that historical reference in pocket.”

Once they were through the process of changing the structure’s zoning from an SRO to a single-family home, Gauthier and Keltner had to contend with its landmarked status, meaning a series of approvals were required for any additions or exterior modifications.

The couple originally planned to construct a three-story extension in the back, but they ended up compromising on two floors. This expansion includes a bump-out for the parlor floor’s kitchen, leading to a rear deck that steps down to the garden. On top, a private terrace connects to the primary bath. “Instead of having that third story, we now have a deck off of our primary suite,” says Keltner. “It’s kind of amazing,” adds Gauthier.

One decision the couple wrestled with mightily was whether to replace the original, 150-year-old pine flooring with ebony-stained white oak. “The floors were part of the reason we liked the house so much,” says Gauthier. But their contractor talked them out of keeping them. “It’s a lot of extra work for them,” says Keltner. “They have to take it up, save it, put something else down, and then put it back. I think we reached a point in time and money where we were like, Okay, fine.”

Another flooring regret was not adding underfoot heating. “I was pushing the mechanical engineers to do radiant heating and radiant cooling,” says Gauthier. “Radiant heat is really lovely—you have warmth under your feet all the time.” But the mechanics behind the system proved complicated and technically redundant, as the home was already configured with a forced-air HVAC system.

“In doing research and other projects, it’s clear that the cost-to-pleasure ratio would have been worth it,” he says now. “We regret not putting the warm floors in.”

While windows might sometimes be an afterthought, Keltner and Gauthier have advice: Go with triple-glazed. “Windows provide heat and cool insulation as well as security,” explains Keltner. “And they can define both the outside and the inside of a space.” The couple went with triple-glazed windows from Austrian-based firm Bewiso that come pre-wired for easy connection to alarm systems. “They’re almost like hurricane windows,” she says.

“You can't break through three panes of glass,” says Gauthier. Due to the building’s landmark status, they had to use historic-style windows on the front facade. “We were able to get these old-fashioned type of windows that when you close them, they feel like you're closing a Mercedes,” says Gauthier. “They completely silence the outdoors.”

The final piece of advice is perhaps the most crucial: “Hire the best contractor you can afford,” says Gauthier.

“The typical advice is to get a few bids and throw out the lowest and the highest,” says Keltner. “People usually take the second lowest.” However, that could be the determining factor between an on-time project and one that drags. “You’ll end up paying the difference both in money and time,” she says. “It’s often hard for people to bite the bullet and go with the slightly higher price, but it really pays off.”

Geoffrey Montes is an associate editor at ELLE Decor with a serious love for all things real estate and design. Before that, he worked at Architectural Digest, Galerie, and Preservation magazines, covering everything from jaw-dropping listings to world-famous architects and design events like Salone del Mobile and Homo Faber.

#6832. Survey Finds 84% of Organizations Reported at Least One AI-Related Incident in the Past Year

Companies are running into problems as they give artificial intelligence more control over critical business applications, with 84% reporting at least one AI-related incident in the past year, according to a new survey from identity security firm Delinea.

The company's 2026 Application Access Governance report found that some organizations have experienced incorrect financial transactions and AI actions they couldn't trace or explain. The findings raise concerns about whether enterprises have enough oversight of AI systems that can make decisions and take actions on their own

The findings are based on an independent survey of 1,000 senior finance and finance technology leaders in the United States and United Kingdom. The AI-specific analysis included 879 respondents whose organizations use the technology in critical business applications.

The report found that 35% of surveyed organizations have deployed AI agents capable of taking actions within business applications. However, only 11% of organizations using AI said their governance and security controls were keeping pace with deployment.

Delinea also found gaps in centralized visibility into agent deployment and permissions, including who owns agents and who approved their use.

Some respondents reported segregation-of-duties conflicts involving AI, while others identified agents that continued operating after the employees who deployed them had left the organization.

The research also highlighted gaps in monitoring access across business systems. Only 46% of respondents said they continuously monitor and manage cumulative access risks across applications.

Those gaps can make it difficult to identify segregation-of-duties conflicts, in which a person or agent has permissions across systems that should be kept separate.

Despite those issues, 95% of respondents said they were confident their access controls would withstand scrutiny in an external audit.

However, 26% reported access-related audit findings that had recurred in consecutive years, while 25% said some findings had remained unresolved for three or more years.

Only 22% of organizations had a single named owner for each application, with ownership tracked in one system of record.

Meanwhile, 60% reported an increase over the past two years in the number of critical applications operating outside the scope of access governance or Sarbanes-Oxley Act compliance.

The findings highlight the oversight challenges associated with giving AI agents authority to act within business systems, particularly when organizations cannot consistently identify who owns an agent or trace its actions.

Frank Vukovits, chief security scientist at Delinea, said organizations remain responsible for the actions AI agents take within their systems.

"When an agent can approve a transaction or change permissions, the business owns the outcome. You need to know what every agent can do, who owns it, and who approved it, before an auditor asks or your financials are misstated," Vukovits said.

For the full report, go to the Delinea site.

Artificial intelligence is saving scientists significant amounts of time, but researchers are giving some of those gains back by checking the technology's work, according to a new study involving Google, Google DeepMind, and MIT.

Our nation's largest early childhood program has not fully caught up with what science now tells us about how children learn.

In an active social engineering campaign, cyber attackers are impersonating IT help desks and using fake passkey setup requests to compromise employee identities and gain access to enterprise cloud data.

Increasingly, cyber criminals are sharing AI prompts, jailbreak techniques, and access to existing models that can be incorporated into fraud and phishing operations, according the latest AI Threat Report from Flashpoint.
